The Royal Wedding at Evesharn.—Qneen Ens of Spain is understood to have acted the part. of match- maker in bringing ahout the marriage of Prince Charles of Bourbon and Princess Louise of Franee. The wedding is to take place next month at Weloti Norton, the splendid place near Evet-hars, in Worcestershire, which the Due &Orleans inherited from his uncle the late Duc d'Arimale. It is expected that the King and Queen of Spain will be present at the wedding in which both the Royal Houses of Spain and Portugal are very nearly interested. The Princess is the fourth and only unmarried sister of the Due d'Orleans, whilst Prince Charles is a widower, he having married the elder sister of King Alfonso, who died some three years ago. The Orleans family are amongst the wealthiest. of ex-Royaltiee, and live in almost regal state at Wood Norton,where Princess Louise spent much of her child- hood. She is tall and stntely-looking,like her sisters the Queen of Portugal, and the Dochpss of Aosta. The people in the neighbourhood of Wood Norton are devoted to her, and the town of Evesharn, some two miles distant, is arranging various fetes in honour of her wedding. -

Women Teachers and Matrimony.—Some commotion has been caused among the young women who teach in our elementary schools, owing to a new departure of the Board of Education, which is likely to have a prejudicial effect upon their matrimonial prospects. This regulation, which is to be rigidly enforced, requires them to enter into a contract to serve as teachers for live years after leaving the training college, Or in the alternative to pay a fine of £2O or £25 for breaking the engagement. Such a rule inflicts no hard- ship upon men who are to be required to serve for seven years. They can marry and go on with their work, but a girl cannot. Usually girl students,ere 21 or 22 before leaving the training college, so that under the new condition of things they will be restrained from marry- ing during the most attractive period of their women- hood.

- - The Board's Point of View.—lt is pretty certain that nubile opinion will not support the Board of Education, In making such a rule operative. It is no doubt reason- able enough from the Board's point of view, which is, that it is entitled to the teacher's services after paying the cost of training the student. But although it may be reasonable. it may not ta expedient. There is too, something to he said on the other side. The student may not contribute in money to theccSt of her training, but she gives her time for some years practically for nothing, during which she might have been earning good wages in some other employment. The conditions are therefore not so one-sided as the Board seems to assume, but on whichever side the balance may he, one injustice is not remedied by retorting to another injustice, and when considered in all its aspects, that is very much what the Board's action amounts to. - •

Spiritual Warnings —A recent incident has served to revive tile question whether there are such things as portents or warnings of death, and in particular whether an audible intimation from the spirit world come 1 within the possibility of belief. There are many storks of ghostly footsteps being heard even without recourse to the legend of the ghost walk in Dickens wonderful book," Bleak House." They all raise the question how z it is possible for a spirit to produce a sound by contact with the floor, or ground, and the average man or woman is apt to recall the old tale of how the narrator of an incredible story saki, " I would not have believed it unless I had seen it." "That is precisely my attitude," said his friend. It is not so easy to dismiss the narratives of persons appearing at the moment of their death to friends at a distance. Many explanations have been adduced with the object of showing that these seeming apparitions could be due to purely mun- dane causes' and it may be so, but at the same time there have been so many occurrences of the kind, authenticated by people of the highest character, and apparently perfectly save in all respects, that one can , scarcely say with complete confidence that no spirit has ever been seen in such circumstances. Dr. Samuel Johnson, at any rate, appears to have had no doubt on the subject, and a great many other people more or less wise, have shared his belief in this kind of apparition. Woman's Talking Powers.—A French professor has come forward to explain why women excel in talking ". powers. This advanta.s is not always referred to in complimentary tfrras t..by the mere man, but it is generally admitted that women can talk faster, aturh longer, and with greater facility, than amen. Probably the great majority of married men would not be disposed to dispute this fact, although of course there are many exceptions, and the explanatien offered is that owing to more adaptable chest and limes powers, Ft woman only fatigues herself to a fourth of the extent that a man does when talking. If that is the case, and it has been tested by numerous experiments, it is fairly strong evidence that there can not he the injurious effects in the corset which many of the agitators on this subject would have us believe.

_ A Candid Kleptomaniac.—An American woman has been giving her vieas on the "divine r.assion " of stealing. She has a husband, rich and willing enough to give her anything that money can buy, and introduce her into the best Society, and yet she cannot help stealing and habitually planning thefts from shops, and from friends houses. *She is just now in gaol, but says that the thrill of pleasure which the successful planning and carrying out of R theft gives her, is more than she can resist. She has been certified to be perfectly sane, and yet says she lives only to come out of prison, and steal again. Usually our Kleptomaniscs are in the last stages of distress when found ont,and money is lavished on legal aid in the endesvonr to escape from the con- sequences of their crime. But the candour of this American who is described as a 3 onng and brilliant woman, suggests that Kleptomania is really one form of that strange perversity of human nature, which prompts us to see but little pleasure in a thing unless there is danger or difficulty in it, ‘ihich makes us want the things that others have, but when once gained, are only too often but as ashes in the mouth. Fra.nds in Fur.—The" faking" of furs has become such a fine art that only experts can be sure against being deceived, end the 'Vigilance Committee-of the Fur ond Skin Trade Section of the London Chamber of Commerce, have issued a circular, warning purchasers against the frauds most, commonly perpetrated by the selling of the manufactured article as genuine fur. ;Mink is very fashionable this auteren. but it is very often only dyed re?rmot, or musquash. These pelts too frequently pose ss sable. Seal and beaver are most skilfully produced from the common furs, and indeed those of the rabbit and the-hare con be turned into almost anything. and are sold as French sable. fox, ermine, chinchilla, and seal, whilst most of the fur that is sold as bear is really goat. Those who are contem- plating the purchase of aim- coat.coller, or stole,should insist upon a full description in the invoice, and if there is any doubt about it, being correctly described, tbit Vigilance Committee will give an expert. opinion, free of charge, if the article is sent to the offices of the Chamber of Commerce, in Oxford Court, Cannon-street, London. - - - -

The Dangerous Fur Fichn.—From a health point of view the fur bolero was bad enough, but the fur richt' , that has made its appearance this autumn is many degrees worse. Two shaped hands of fur cross the shoulders anti join V fashion the waist behind ; in the i front the bands also come together, then fall stole fashion almost to the feet. Such a wrip is graceful : and artistic in the extreme,imt one of the most dangerous ' we have ever yet had offered us. It covers the back and chest partially, leaving the other portions exposed in such a manner as to court bronchitis and pneumonia every time we wear it. MADGE. . I

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

FEATHER BOAS AND MUFFS

Many chilly women want a really voluminous and warm stole or boa and .muff for cold

weather, but cannot afford good furs, and will not wear cheap ones. The feather boa and muff en suite I:as ben slowly, if steadily, growing in favour for the last three winters, and it is rather a wonder, says a Queen writer, it has not become more popular, and so done to death, before this. It is certainly becoming. and by no means extra- vagant; in fact, very pretty sets can be obtained nowadays in a variety of feathers and colourings for a most modest sum. These sets wear far better than one would expect, and I can speak from experience that they are quite as durable as many kinds of fur, and, with an occasional shaking out and warming before a fire, look well for a very long time.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
DRESSING-GOWN FOR WINTER

In our sketch is depicted a useful and cosy dressing-gown for winter wear. There are many suitable materials that it can be made up in, such, for instance, as fine French flannel, Molle- ton flannel, or a heavy make of cashmere. For ordinary wear a rather deep shade of cornflower- blue might be chosen, trimmed with bands of blue-and-white embroidered galon. The gown is

a simpie sack shape, with a tucked yoke deep on the shoulders back and front, and fastening over on the left side beneath the band of trim- ming. The trimming outlines the neck, and is carried along the top of the shoulders and down the outside of the sleeves. The sleeves are full and of three-quarter length, and are drawn into loose-fitting bands of embroidered galon.

THE HUGUENOT HAT.

Some entirely new hats have put in an appear- ance, together with some models that reproduce the absurdities of the past few months, with their extravagant brims and their excessive trim- ming. One of the newest shapes is the " Huguenot," with a high crown and a rather narrow brim. Most of the hats, says P. T. 0., are over-trimmed, some being topped with enor- mous bows of ribbon or velvet, and others being crowded with plumes of a most elegant and ex- pensive variety. Again, birds and wings are seen, but always to an exaggerated extent; some hats having as many as six large-sized wings starting from • a chou in front and overhanging both crown and brim. The hat with the wide brim turned up shapely in front is also seen; and ipually this hat has its brim smartly faced with black moire, silk, or satin in a fetching and be- coming way. Many of the hats shewn are ab- surdities, but as nothing is obligatory, the busi- ness of the sensible woman will be to select a pretty new hat with which tQ greet theautumn days.

TEACHERS AND MARRIAGE.

The young women who teach in our elemen- tary schools are a highly interesting class of the community, and by the excellence' as well as the importance of their work they have established a claim to the gratitude of the nation. Sweet and gracious is their influence in the schools. Some- thing in the nature of their work—its constant call on the sympathies and the intelligence— helps to enhance their tex attractiveness. A lady teacher in the first flush of womanhood is a very fascinating young creature. But good as her marriage prospects have hitherto been, we fear, says the Doily Chronicle, that they are likely to suffer if a new rule laid down by the Board of Education is to be rigidly enforced.

"No, Ralph." she retorted, smiling up at him. "I should never have suspected you of such-- humility! Why, sir, you always treated me alkif I were the inferior—as I was, as I was, and always shall be, dearest!" "But Veronica," he said, gravely, and after a reuse, "I may be, I feel that I am, the Earl's soli, but my claim has not been proved. There • is Mr. Talbot Denby; he is the next heir, you know, and he will have something to say to it. He does not bear me any goodwill, Veronica, and he will no doubt dispute my claim • indeed, he will be justified in doing so." "But he does not," said Veronica. "I have just seen him, and he spoke as if he admitted your identity and did not intend to dispute it." Ralph frowned thoughtfully. "That's strange," he said. "Mr. Talbot Denby has no liking for me—you remember our little set-to by the river ? " _ _

_ "Do you think I forget anything concerning you?" she murmured, her head resting on his breast, her eyes, with love beaming from them, turned up to his. "It is strange that he should so readily, so promptly resign the earldom. An earldom ! Heavens ! I can scarcely realise it. But lam glad, dearest, for if I should be an Earl, you will be a Countess and that is a fitting rank for you. Ah !" he broke off sharply, "here I am talking of the future as if it were all bright and assured, and—and—" he looked round the cell.

Womanlike her courage rose to cheer his momentary despondency. "It will not be far long, Ralph," she whispered. "The truth will prevail." "After—when it is too late," he said, more to himself than to her; then, as if ashamed of his doubts and fears he added quickly: "Yes, yes, that's a good motto, dearest. The truth will prevail." There was a pause, then she said, hesitatingly: "You did not know that I was out that—that night, Ralph, the night of the—the night you left?"

"No," he assented, absently. "Yes, I was. I went to the hut. I went to see you, to tell you that I could not let you go, surrender you. I saw Burehett and learnt you had gone. And—l saw someone else." "Yes ?" ho said. "Who was it ? " "It was Talbot. Ho was walking from the wood."

Ralph nodded. "Yes?" he said. "He was taking an after-dinner stroll. He saw no one, I

suppose?" "I have not asked him," replied Veronica in a low voice, "for just now he told me—he volunteered the information—that he had not left the terrace that night." Ralph nodded. "He had forgotten, I suppose," he said, still absently. ' "You think it is of little, of no consequence ? " he asked. _ _ _

He shook his head. "Why should it be, dearest ?" he said. "If he had met anyone ho would have mentioned it You are not thinking there can. be any connection between Talbot Denby and the murder, are you ? " he added, with a grave smile. "How could there be? They could not have known each other, could have had no reason for quarrelling." Veronica shook her head. "No, I see there can be no connection; but, oh Ralph, every little thing, light and trivial and irrelevant as it may seem, has weight for me. And why did he say he did not leave the terrace that night, When I saw him—l saw himitin the wood ? ' Ralph shrugged his shoulders. "Can't say," he responded. 'But don't let us think any more of this terrible murder, Veronica. I want to talk about yourself, what you are doing, whether you are keeping well. You look pale and harassed. Dearest, you will be ill. And that will be the worst misfortune that can happen to me. My dear, brave-hearted darling, remember 'Truth will prevail.' "

The fact that the great Earl of Lynborough had acknowledged Ralph Farringdon as his son, and heir ran through the county like quicksilver. penetrated to London, and, of course, intensified - the interest which the Lynborough murder case was exeiting. At the second examination the great London newspapers sent their special reporters, and the small court-house was crammed with people, while an excited mob surged and overflowed the street in which the building stood, so that the eminent counsel from town and other persons engaged in the case had to•• fight their way in. Though Dr. Thorne and Talbot, and even Veronica, had implored the Earl to absent him- self he had driven down to the court and had taken a seat just below the Bench, for it would not have been seemly for him to sit upon the Bench itself. The appearance of tho bent, frail figure, with its white and haggard face, from which the dark eyes shone with a half- pathetic,. half-fierce and defiant expression, caused a "sensation" in the packed court. But the old man seemed quite unconscious of the interest and excitement his presence aroused, and leaning back in,his chair fixed his eyes, on the dock as if he were waiting for the appear-- ance of the prisoner. Presently Ralph came in with a couple of con-. stoles by his side. He was pale, but perfectly v.lf-possessed, and he looked round the crowded plave with composure until his eyes rested on the figure in the chair below the Bench; then for a moment his calm broke up, a look of tender- ness and pity shone in his eyes, and his lips quivered. It was for a moment only; the next ho enlm and self-possessed again, and he stood with folded arms and head erect with an air of patient attention. "Yes, he'ilike the Earl," ran round tho packed court. "He holds himself like his lordship. And look at his eyes and mouth, they be the. very same exactly, and no one can deny it!" Talbot, from his seat beside the Earl, heard the• various murmurs, and his sallow face grew still' n ore sallow.

The usual formal evidence was produced, then' Inspector Grey said: "I will now call the witness who saw the prisoner on the night of the murder. Fanny Mason!" Talbot started slightly, but controlled his emotion and forced his face into an expression' of grave interest only. "Fanny Mason ! " echoed the usher. There was a stir in the court, then an intense silence as Fanny was conducted by a policeman• to the witness-box.

(To be coniinu4d.)

A CURE FOR MOPING,

A few years ago Sir Lauder Brunton startled and amused us by announcing that he frequently gave his patients "temper powders," aqd that these were very useful things to keep in the house. He has now come forward with another household prescription, which is designed to lengthen our days. and should, therefore, says the Lady's Pictorial, be exceedingly popular, especi- ally as it is a pleasant one. It is primarily a cure for ennui, and .is, therefore, given in all cases where patients declare that they do not know what to do with themselves, and are obvi- ously suffering from imaginary ills, but those who follow Sir Lauder Brunton's prescription may also certainly count on extension of life. The "temper powders" of this eminent physi- cian are actually procurable "at all chemists," but his later prescription has to be "made up" at home, for it is nothing more or less than a dose of stamp-collecting. Take up this craze, and you will never be dull; with an occupied mind and a hobby to keep you alert, you will take a new lease of life.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

LESION TARTLETS.—First line some p"tty- pans with short crust. Then blend a heaping tablespocrnful of flour smoothly with a little cold milk; pour over it nearly half a pint of boiling milk • and add 4oz. of sugar and 2oz. Of raisins, stoned and chopped fine; simmer gently until the fruit and the flour are thoroughly cooked. Take off the fire, and when cool add to the in- gredients the beaten yolks of two eggs, one tablespoonful of slightly-warmed butter, and the grated rind and the juice of one lemon. Fill the prepared patty-pans with the mixture, and bake in a moderate oven. When nearly cooked. put a meringue on eacn tamer, Inane uy wiliss.- ing the whites of the eggs to a stiff froth and mixing with it one tablespoonful of fine sugar. Sprinkle over some finely-chopped almonds, and return to tho oven to brown thoroughly.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS

Dr. Harold Joseph Resigns.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday undo the presid- ency of Mr. W. Houghton. There were also present : Rev. T. R. Matthews, Rev. M. Ryan, Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Simpson, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, W. Gill, R. Bolton, W. Furness' W. C. Brooks, W. Flannagan, G. Mather, A. Brooks, M. W. Seton, J. C. button (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master) and A Wright (superintendent of the Cottage Homes).

THE FINANCIAL POSITION. AN' ADVERSE BALANCE. The CLERK submitted an important statement on the fi nanial position of the Union. He stated that the balance in the treasurer's hands on September 30th, 1906, was £5,333 195. 7d., and on September 30th, 1907, £2,293 16s. 9d. showing a fall of £3,040 2s. 10d. The treasurer's balance on October 17th, 1906, was £4,16365., the quarterly bills totalled £2,702 4s. id. leaving a favourable balance of £1,461 2s. 11d. The treasurer's balance at present was £1,625 15s. 5d., the quarterly bills totalled £3,593 18a. 10d., showing that whereas in October, 1906, a surplus of £1,461 2s. lid. remained, after payment of accounts for which cheques were that day drawn, an adverse balance of £1,908 Zs. sd. would result. Mr. A. BitooKs : Are we to look forward to being sold up? (Laughter.) The CHAIRMAN don't know. The CLERK explained that he had made very early calls upon the overseers. Of course, he must rely upon IVarrington and one or two of the larger parishes to enable him to pay the accounts. He was only afraid that, if the expenditure was to continue at the rate it appeared to be goingf they would not only require the extra penny they put on the rates but that they would have to spend the whole of the grants which came in this half year and upon which he relied when he did not suggest Bd. instead of 741. These grants were ordinarily made t' spread over two half years. They began with an adverse balance of £2,000 and the question was whether the grants and the 7d. would have to be expended during the half-year. RESIGNATION OF DR. HAROLD JOSEPH, The CLERK read a letter from Dr. Harold Joseph asking him to brine enclosed letter of resignation before the Board of 'Guardians at the earliest oppor-. tunity. The l!stter, dated October 13th, natd as follows' —To the Chairman of the Boarl of Guardians. Dear Sir,—l beg to send in my resignation of the post of Deputy Medical °Meer to the IVarrington Workhouse. I should like to take this opportunity of thanking the members of the Board whom I have from time to time met for their kindness and courtesy towards me, and I am sorry I cannot extend the same thanks to all the members of the staff and to certain persons, not on the staff, who seem to have some undefined connection with the working of the institution. I shall continue to perform the duties of my office until such time as the Board elect my successor, if you wish me to do so. Believe me. sir, yours faithfully, HAROLD JOSEPH. Mr. MATHER:: That letter comes as a surprise to me and no doubt to everybody in the room, and I suggest it be referred to the House Committee to investigate The CLERK: I think you must accept the resigna- tion. The CHAIRMAN: I was going to suggest that yet accept the resignation and leave the letter to be dealt with by the House Committee. If Dr. Joseph chooses to resign, of course Mr. MATHER : Yes, hut in the letter there is more between the lines. The CHAIRMAN: The least said about it at present the better. Mr. MATHER : I don't want to move that the resignation be accepted. I want to leave it unaccepted. Mr. W. C. BROOKS : I shall support Mr. Mather in proposing that it be referred to the House Com- mittee. The CHAIRMAN: The main poiut of the letter refers to Dr. Joseph's resignation and I take it we have no option but to accept it. The CLERK advised that the Board should formally accept the resignation on the understanding that Dr. Joseph would continue in office until the appointmen- of his successor and leave any other points to the cont sideration of the Rouse Committee. Mr. A. BROOKS: If there is any feeling on the part of Dr. Joseph and if that feelint, can be got over, it is quite competent for the House 'Committee to arrange with Dr. Joseph to withdraw his resignation. The CLERK: The doctor can withdraw it. Mr. MATHER said it was very unpleasant to contem- plate the resignation of Dr. Joseph whom he regarded as a rising medical man. After the clerk's ruling, how- ever, he would move that the resignation be accepted with regret and that the letter be referred to the House Committee. Mrs. SIMPSON seconded and the motion was agreed to. Mr. JOLLEY suggested that the House Committee be empowered to take such action as might be considered necessary to secure a successor. Mr. FURNESS : I think Oa is rather premature, Mr. JOLLEY : Other vise you don't give the House Committee any power at all. Mr. Fuaxess : I take it in any case if they thought flt, the plwer would be delegated to them. On the suggestion of the CHAIRMAN it was agreed that the committee should report on the matter at the next meeting of the Board. HOUSE COMMITTEE. At a meeting of the House Committee held on October 4th a vote of condolence was passed with the resident medical officer on the death of his father. It was resolved to rcornmend that an additional assistant male lunatic attendant be enraged at a salary of £25 yearly together with uniform, apartments and washing. Further that the master obtain from the National Telephone Company an approximate cost for the establishment of inter-telephonic com- munication in the House, and also for an exchange centre. The question of increasing the number of charge nurses was deferred. At a meeting on October 11th the Special Committee's recommendation relative to the appointment pf a third lunatic attendant in order to effecti an eight hours shift was considered and unanimously agreed to, the salary to he £25 per annum, uniform and indoor allowances and when this minute is confirmed by the Board the special committee be authorised to purchase all neces- sary furniture for his quarters.—The Master reported the circumstance.. attending the death of a child named Kelly, and the delay in notifying the Coroner. The minutes were confirmed with the exception of the portion relating to the increase of staff, which will be placed on the agenda for the next Board meeting. APPLICATION FOR SALARY INCREASE. gr. BASIL SHEARER wrote asking fora revision of his salary as assistant relieving officer. He stated that 27 years ago he was appoirfted assistant relieving officer at a salary of £5O. Some few years afterwards be was appointed school attendance officer for a portion of the Rural District of the Union 'tit a salary of £25. This position be held until about seven years ago when the Guatlians at his request allowed him to give up the position and gener- °lndy added the salary to his appointment as assistant relieving officer. The duties he had to undertake had increased very considerably during the past few years. He was also enquiry officer under the Education Act at a salary of VO per annum and lie wati collector for the Guardians with a commission of 10 per cent. His income from the latter appointment was decreasing each year through no fault of his own. His district comprised the whole of the Union for all appoint- ments and consequently his expenses were considerable. The letter will be considered at the next meeting.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington's prowess as cup-fighters was again demonstrated on Saturday when, with a team short of several regular backs, they went to Runcorn and beat the locals in handsome fashion in the first round for the Lancashire Cup. I doubt whether anyone expected Warrington to win. Even with a full team the task of beating Runcorn away generally proves too much for them, and when Fish, Brouks, and Harris cried off at the last minute the chances of Warrington appeared very faint indeed. In a League match, they would probably have lost but as the game was a cup-tie they buckled to in grim earnest and beat the Linnets fore and aft by l 5 points to 2 points.

The drew for the second round of the Lancashire Cup was made on Monday night as follows : Widnes v. Brought .n Rangers Leigh v. Warrington • Salford v. Wigan Oldham v. Swinton To be played on October 26th.

Much of the credit for Warrington's victory falls to the forwards, who realised what was due from them and fairly paved the way for success. They were the old brigade, which has done so well before in Cup-ties,and they used their weight to some purpose. Runcorn have long been on the light side in the scrummage, and Warrington played on this weakness very effectively. The conditions also favoured a forward game, rain' having made the, ground and the hall very wet, and the wirepullers fairly excelled in a style of play usually foreign to them Chief credit for the victory is certainly due to the scrummagers, who were led in great style by Thomas. The Welshman played one of finest games of his career.

It must not be thought that the hacks were no good, however. They played well without exception, and , their fielding and punting of the greasy ball was ' really fine. Tilley set a great example at full back, and seems to have quite got back to the form which gained him fame at Widnes. IKenyon was the greatest success of the I day, and the way he scored two of the three tries • proved that he is no worse for a season's rest. He has a great opportunity now, for the Warrington team is sadly in need of a class wing in addition to Fish. Kenyon seemed to prove on Saturday that there is no need to look much farther. He was both strong and speedy. Dickenson was better in the centre than on the wing, but is not running well, and Cartwright , again held his own. Isherwood was reliable as ever, and the halves fair, Jenkins shining most on the defence.

Runcorn are having a very rough time this season, and one victory out of six matches is indicative of a surprising loss of form. The team is much the same as last season, when the League Championship was almost within reach, but the men are shaping poorly. The forwards have lost their vim, and cut up badly on Saturday, while the stalwarts like Butterworth and Jolley and Walker are not the men they were. They have apparently reached the veteran age. Harrison was certainly much missed on Saturday, for Jolley would have been far more valuable at the base of the scrum and he was caught napping when Dickenson scored the first try. But ! Moran and Deakin were terribly weak and a long way below last season's form. Still the Linnets are far, from being a hopeless side and if they could unearth two or three forwards of the Harry Farmer type I am convinced they would begin winning matches with some regularity. There is much latent cleverness in the side yet, though it had poor opportunities for dis- play on Saturday.

Runcorn had a capital gate, and I was atnazed at the loyalty of the Warrington supporters. Notwith- standing the rain and the absentees from the chosen side,' there must have been quite 1,500 of them there. They swelled • the home club's returns in a way tliat made some compensation for the heavy defeat.

So satisfied were the Warrington -Committee with the display of the team at Runcorn that the same side has been chosen with the sole exception that Brooks • takes the place of Jenkins. The team is : They, full back ; Kenyon, Isherwood, Dickenson and Cart- wright, threequarters ; Lees and Brooks, half backs ; Thomas, Heath, Boardman, Shugars, Belton and .1 Harmer forwards.

Fish is said to he nursing a damaged wrist, but his neglect of training is getting too serious for the com- mittee to tolerate. When is the champion swerver going to begin getting his weight under 12 stones?

The "A "team to play against Widnes " A " to-day will be: Hewitt, full back ; Jordan, Landor, Lloyd, and Harrison, threequarters ; Jenkins and Daintith, halfbacks; Jolley, Boardman, Forster, Bradshaw, Holbrook and Fairhurst, forwards.

Warring.ton will have a hard game to-day at Widnes, or I am much mistaken. The Chemicals struck me as being a greatly improved side on Wednesday when facing the New Zealanders, and some of their running and passing was really smart. Forwards they are as vigorous as ever, and the War- rington vanguard will have a severer task than last week.

THE ALL BLACKS AT WIDNES.

The New Zealanders are still winning. They went to Widnes on Wednesday and beat the locals comfort- ably by 4 goals 6 tries (26 points) to 1 goal 3 tries (11 points). Though Widnes had the honour of scoring the greatest rmakber of points against the All Blacks so far recorded, they r ere quite outclassed in pace and combination.. (Their third try, scored right on time, was a "gilt "—and due to the carelessness of the defence —but thoge scored by Barber and Lambert were the outcome of pretty football and orthodox half and three-quarter play.

If the New Zealanders have a bad fault it is that common to most Rugby Unionists they give their opponents too much rope on the attack. 'rhe "All Blacks" are not the equals of the best Northern Union teams in the gentle art of "spotting," and their defence leaves much to be desired. On the attack they are superb, but I never- theless think they are in some slight danger at Broughton to-day. They will meet a team of different class to Bramley, Huddersfield, anil Widnes, whereas they themselveS have not yet settled down to the. Northern Union game.

For instance, at Widnes they bled another experi- ment in the shape of playing seven forwards, one-half, four threequarters, and a full back. In the scrums they naturally did better,. and the speed and cleverness of the men behind was sufficient to make up for the reduced number. Only occasionally did the absence. of a seventh back seem to handicap the All Blacks, and that was when the home lialves and threequarters got well on the run. Then nothing but. superior pace saved the situation, and twice the Widnesians broke through. I wonder how Warrington would fare to-day if they played the same formation. With only one half,.l am afraid the threequarters would starve. They certainly would not score the points. the

The Great Secret of Curing Skin Diseases

Anointing the skin to heal its injuries and sores and keep the tissues healthy and free from blemish is a very ancient custom of Rome. The skin is the great purifying agent of the human system. It is pierced by millions of tiny holes or pores which breathe out the impurities of the system, and it is generally when these pores are not working properly, or clogged up by the use of course salves or ointments that sores and eruptions break out. Is it to be wondered at that the very words ointment and salve to-day, ass,-ciated as they are with rancid animal grease and mineral substitutes are repulsive to the majority of people? With the advent of Zam-Buk, famed for its refinement and marvellous curative properties, ointments and salves are now being entirely discarded. Zam-Bak owes its origin to scientific thought in an entirely new direction. It is a reversion to the custom of anointing the skin, prevailing in the days of ancient Rome, when the secret of an unblemished skin was appreciated, and when the brave gladiators so seldom emerged from the arena without some gaping wound, that rapid and natural skin-healing was vital to his safety. In the scientific inquiry that led up to the won- derful discovery of Zam-Buk, two practical con- clusions were come to, Viz. : (1) That animal fats and mineral poisons were an unnatural, unclean, and harmful innova- tion in the ointments of modern times, that oint- ments and salves employing such ingredients could not, on account of their lack of refinement, be taken up by the pores of the skin; whileif any of the nasty grease did burrow into the flesh, no real good, but possibly harm was likely to follow. (2) That inasmuch 38 Nature herself first gave us the instinct to rub a place that hurts, the ideal application was probably to be found in amongst Nature's rare medicinal herbs and plants growing on the outskirts of civilization. , How successful were the inquirieft rating these lines—bow certain vegetable saps and juices and oils were carefully selected and then how costly experiments became necessary in order to refine these products, and ascertain the best proportion of each beautiful ingredients- how when the healing, soothing, and antiseptic efficiency of the pure balm had been attrined, there still remained the task of inventing new and large enough machinery for producing Zain- J3uk on a scale and at a price within reach of the most modest purse ; and bow since the attain- ment of this success Zam-Buk has justified its claim to be the world's greatest healing balm, and as such is to-day freely endorsed by medical men,, nurses, and others, and is the ever-ready first-aid and skin-cure of the British race —This story is one of the wonders of the 20th century. Zam-Buk has revolutionised existing methods of healing cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, chapped hands, cold-sores chafing, in treating in a new scientific and effective way the worse case of eczema, piles, ulcers, bad legs, blood poisoning, festering, psoriasis, scurf, , ringworm, childen's scalp troubles, soles on the back, and all those inflamed, burning, irritable and diseased condi- tions of the human skin that incapacitate and torture their thousand victims Zam-Buk draws disease out of the skin and tissues ; it kills microbes, and ends all inflamma- tion and festering; and after purifying a wound or sore, grows new healthy skin in a quick and marvellous manner. PROPOSED RECREATION GROUND.

Stockton Heath Scheme Discussed.

The latest subject of discussion at Stockton Heath is the suggestion that the Parish Council should acquire a recreation ground for the use of the children of the village. Since the matter was first introduced at the Parish meetiug in March the Council has-made inauiries and the site which appeared to commend itself was a plot of five and a half acres, in Fairfield-road, which Messrs. Greenell, Whitley and Co., offered at £lOO per acre. The firm, however, intimated their willingness to contribute a dona;ion of £lOO to the scheme, thus reducing the cost to £450. As part of the land is in Latchford Without it was decided to invite the co-operation of the Council of that parish, and they, through their chairman, Mr. George Pilling, have offered to give £2OO, less their proportion of the brewery companies offer—lo—towards the initial cost, leaving the subsequentmaintenance charges entirely to Stockton Heath. On Tuesday eveniug a meeting of ratepayers of Stockton Heath was held in the Hall Cafe to discuss the situation. Mr. J. W. Bonchier presided over a large attendance. The Chairman said the Parish Council did not see how the upkeep of the ground could be undertaken on less than £lOO a year. That would mean, in addition to the first cost, something like 2d. in the ;or the total cost of purchasing the land, laying it. out and the upkeep might possibly reach 3d. in - the R. It was for the meetiag to consider that and say whether they were in favour of going on with the scheme or not. lie would remind them that within the next eighteen months they would have new schools built in Stockton Heath, and be did not see how they were going to have them pro- vided at less than fonrpence or fivepence in the g,which they would have to face in addition if they went on with the recreation ground. _ _ . .

A long discussion ensned, in which opinion was divided as to the suitability of the site, some ratepayers contending that it was not central enough. N either was there unanimity as to whether the whole of the ground should be used as a pla3 ground pure and simple or part of it should be laid out as a park. The general feeling, however, appeared to be that the offer of Latchford Without was not sufficiently generous, as they would share the advantages of the park and ought also io bear part of the annual mainten- ance charges. One speaker who advocated another site and was told that it would be too expensive, advocated the postponement of the scheme6intil the Government had introduced the Land Valuation Act, under which they would probably be able to get ground at a reasonable price. Another suggestion which met with some accep- tance was that when the new school was erected the play- ground should be thrown open to the children after school hours, but there was considerable doubt as to whether the authorities would approve of that course. Eventually it was decided to defer the matter, for the further consideration of the Parish Council. Prior to the Parish meeting the Parish Council met under the presidency of Mr. Bouchier and came to the conclusion that the offer of Latchford Without Parish Council was not sufficiently generous.

tALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] THE OFFER OF A THRONE

BY ATHOL FORBES,

Author of "Cassock and Comedy," "A Son of Rimmon," "Odd Fish," "A Clerical Cupid," "Bashful Eighteen," "A Girl of Moods," &c.

"A great fuss is always made in the Press about a nation's honour and a country's dig- nity. Neither the one nor the other really exists; there is not even the proverbial honour- among thieves. It is no high moral feeling that puts the brake on the exercise of power by one nation to crush another, but the fear of the other's strength. Of course, when another nation has to climb down, the ques- tion of dignity is introduced, but the crisis is over then." The ex-Ambassador had unbended. We were in the billiard-room of Elsdon %Castle, discussing the Fashoda business, and one of the younger Members of Parliament present was putting forward a plea for France. "Yes," continued his Excellency, "it is only the fear that the other nation will get the best of it which holds back the dogs of war. It is a grand thing in diplomacy, this suspicion, this want of trust-" Surely, Duke, you cannot mean it ? " put in our hostess.

"I do mean it," he replied, with convinc- ing emphasis. "I mean, of course, it is e great thing for this country. If nations could only trust each other, why, England mould be confronted with an overpowering alliance that would reduce her to a fourth-rate Power in less than six weeks. Of course, we all have the god or goddess ' Honour ' on our tongue ends, but that is for the mob. Democracy likes the sound of such words, but as regards nations, we diplomatists know how meaning- less such expressions are. A Miuidter gets up in the House, and expresses 'a confident hope' that matters will be arranged in a way agreeable to the traditional honour and dig- nity of the nation. At the same time, our re- presentatives abroad are lying in a %sty that would bring a blum to th-_, countenance of 'he, arch-fiend himself. Why, when I think of the dreadful lies I have told in order to keep the peace of Europe-" "You haven't had any coffee," exclaimed our host to the Vicar, who was preparing to remonstrate warmly. " Ah, thanks, yes," replied the reverend gent'.man, sitting 4• again su.tdenly. The fact was, we were all anxious to hear the ex-Ambassador, and we kicked coldly on the Vicar's contribution to the conversation, which might possibly take his Excellency away upon a side issue. Our host watched his reverence like a terrier watching a rat, and succeeded in forestalling him each time. " Talk about Jesuits," continued the ex- Ambassador, "they are not in it with the up- to-date diplomat. Take Lord Shuffleton, for example, who is a prominent figure-head of so many religious societies, and a frequent speaker at Exeter Hall. Why, this disease, or whatever you like to term it, of lying had laid such hold upon him that he could not tell the truth, or write the simple facts of a case even to his own Government, without insert- ing. the most outrageous falsehoods

T ` I am sure you -are not comfortable in that chair, Vicar," broke in our host again. "Let me pull this one - " "I am quite comfortable, I thank you," snapped the reverend gentleman, whose looks certainly contradicted his assertion. "Do you really mean that England could be invaded ? " suddenly demanded our hos- tess, by way of .getting the ex-Ambassador into some narrative, and relieving the feelings of the Vicar.

"This country has had a narrow squeak of being invaded on more occasions than one in my experience. It is only, as I said befoi e, the mutual distrust which saves us from it."

"What about our Navy t " asked a member of the company, whose earlier life had been spent in the senior service. The old Duke smiled good-naturedly, with the air of a man who has been interrupted by a imestion put to him by a child of tends. years: _ _

"My dear sir, wet have only just got a Navy. I am speaking of the last fifty years; besides, I still adhere to my original assertion. It is distrust of one's neighbour that pre- vents the unloosing of the arch-fiend upon this country." "Do you mean .to say there are no higher motives ? ' cried the Vicar, in his best pulpit tones.

"Not in the world of diplomacy, sir. Strength and, self-interest are the only mo- tives. No higher qualities than these move nations, as a rule. Certainly no philan- thropic scruples will deter them from taking us by surprise when they have consummated plans for our ruin. Leading articles indulge in these moral platitudes, but diplomatists cannot afford 'them. If once two European nations agreed to trust each other the problem of invading England is not a difficult one.!"

"1 nevei: reaa a scheme yet that had for its object the landing of a force in this country that did not carry impossibility on the face of it. Their difficulty, hitherto insurmountable, is mobilisation," said the naval man.

"That is very likely, but it does not mean that a scheme cannot be formed. You may have read much, but let me tell you of a little incident that you have not read, when this island of ours was within an ace of an assault upon its coasts, which, to my mind, had tre- mendous odds in favour of its success. You are a naval man. Perhaps you will acknow- ledge the truth of the axiom- laid down by one of the greatest strategists of the day, 'Forty- eight hours' start ir. modern Aver means vic- tory.'" The naval _man nodded, and looked wise.

"Very well. You are aware that the great Powers of Europe bold annual manceuvres, for which they' mobilise a Certain number of ships, in addition to those already in commis- sion. These vessels are fitted out on war footing, ready in every .way for action. No suspicion is ever aroused in this Country by such preparations; they are taken as a matter of course, and thought 'nothing of. The 'Press, if hard up for news, notices them, but that is about all, and the ordinary English- man _sleeps calmly in his bed, never idrerthaning of anything like danger. The French mameuvres, if my memory fail me net, are. held in July, a little before ours. The Russian and German come on a little later., All these fleets could remain in commission, without causing the least alarm, or attracting more. than passing notice among us. Thus they could have their maximum strength available when our fleet is reduced to a minimum, or may be away on some wild-goose maneeuvres in the neighbourhood of the Azores. ,Thus the difficulty of mobilisation is no difficulty at all. At least/once in each year allies trusting' each other could make a sudden descent upon us, with weapons already sharpened, when. ours have been temporarily laid aside—per- haos our chief ships in dock." The naval man looked serious. The Vicar nodded vigorously, and .he involuntarily leant. forward to hear more.

"You are qua° a strategist," said our host. "I am not. What I have put before you formed part of a plot to ruin this country, which, thank heaven, we were able to frustrate before it was put into practice."__

"Do tell us all about it," urged our hostess, as the speaker paused to light a cigar. "It is not so long ago, either. And, believe me, that iiii„me plot .will be attempted agitin.":

" Heaven forbid " said the V icar,solemnly. "You see, the advantage is with the ag- gressor; if it be true that forty-eight hours' start means victory. England will not be the aggressor, for she has too much to lose, but when we say that, we also assert in the same breath that our. enemies have. much to win. If the 'Whole of the French fleet were sunk, .it would not interfere with the pleasure, say, of Paris, because each Parisian knows We cannot attempt an invasion • with our small Army. But you will perceive that the case stands very differently in regard to us. France and Germany will have on their side- the incalcul- able advantage; they can choose the date and the mode of assault when the thunderbolt shall fall. They can 'lay their plans for a definite date, see that all machinery is in thorough order. put on ;Allard coal. ammuni- tion—in a word, inoott ?se without causing any suspicion."

‘`And this was actually done?" gasped our host.

"Yes. France and Russia were the actual

Utters, but who conceived the scheme is not known. Contemporaneously with the French manceuvres, the Russian fleets were to exer- cise with their ships in the Baltic. A note was to be served simultaneously on our repre- sentatives at St. Petersburg and Paris, de- manding the instant evacuation of_ Egypt. At the same moment the Muscovite fleet was to pass the Dardanelles, to effect a junction with their allies in the Mediterranean. While this,* was being carried out, the Baltic fleets were to join the French in the North Sea. A sledge- hammer blow was to be dealt to our fleet in the Mediterranean before they could be made aware that hostilities had begun. Prepara- tions had been made to disconnect England in her telegraphic communication with the Con- tinent and her Colonies, and to block the Suez Canal. This was the plot, and in my opinion it had, at that period, tremendous odds upon it, for those nations had come to an un- derstanding. Before we could have realised what had taken place, some of our chief ports would have fallen a prey to the assailants. Unsuspecting ships in all directions would have been captured or sunk." "What a fiendish plot ! " said our hostess, in a terrified whisper. ' "Fiendish ! Well, that is our view. They considered it rather fine. It all depends- on the angle of vision. The, other day the Japs got in their blow first with the Russians, and in one night the question of naval supremacy was settled. The Czar called it an act of foul treachery ; the Japs considered it a clever strategy. The world is on the side of the Japanese. But to , return to this _plot."

‘-` How was it deiected ? " asked- several.

" Umph ! " ejaculated the ex-Ambassador, who paused to puff his cigar. "I am sure you were the man who frus- trated it," cried our hostess. The ex-Ambassador smiled, and then tiled to look as modest as he could under a com- pliment. He continued: "Our suspicions were aroused by the fre- quent conferences between the representa- tives of these two Powers. We knew that some treachery was abroad, and the mystery caused us many an hour's unrest, but for weeks we could not scent it out. At length a German I diplomatist put me on the trail, though he denied any knowledge of the plot on the part of his Government. 'I do not know what it is,' he said to me, 'but Prince can tell you all.' We lost' no time in getting hold of him. Though a prince, he was living from hand to mouth, and treated with scant courtesy by the European Courts— you see, there are so many or them—but we managed him. In nearly all these things it is a question of price, and what on earth to offer, and how to offer it, was the puzzle. My Government is very stingy in the way of gifts. That is one of the drawbacks to British diplo- macy. However, I had to tackle the Prince, while I did not forget to drop hints 'in tain quarter that the opposivion in France had made certain overtures to us. This aroused the demon of suspicion in the bre43t of the Muscovite bear. It was amusing to s..e the whispered conferences between the Freti.il and Russian Attaches. At that time Greece wanted a King. I called upon Prince —, and in confidence told him that, although he must not regard it as official, yet I could in- timate to him without a breach of confidence that he was thought of by my Government as a suitable man for the vacant throne.- He snapped- at the bait, poor chap.. He knew he would be able to wipe off some old scores with those who had snubbed him. I watched his face. To the expression of joy there succeeded one of gloom. 'Russia is not with us in our choice, I may inform you, but we have Ger- many, and expect Frsince to consent,' I said."

Here he paused, while our host replenished his glass. "That interview is the most memorable one in my life," he said, gravely, after a sip of h;s whisky. "Thank heaven, I did not know the gravity of the situation until afterwards, for I needed all my nerve. I ,can see Prince now. I can recall the play of countenance as he wrestled with his secret. •He knew it would wreck all his hopes if allowed to suemetl, and he could put us on our guard by a wor.l. The temptation of a throne • was too groat. In less than ten minutes from the time I made my communication I was in possession of the plot, with all its details. That year there were no naval manceuvres. Every hour was spent cobbling up our ships, and nice old rat- traps some of them were. Naval estimates followed, with the present rosult, a large in- crease to the Navy, but not a hint of the real reason ever got into print, or war would have been inevitable."

"Did he get the throne of Greece?" asked the Vicar.

The ex-Ambassador smiled knowingly. "Au. you want me to tell you the name

"Then he did," our hostess called out sharply; "and I know the name."

"Na, he did not. He refused it."

"Refused it !" we ejaculated. "Ye. After the affair was over. I pointed out to him that suspicion would fall upon him as the man who gave away the plot, if England persisted in his candidattire, for the nations would wonder what hi., claim rested upon, and' it only required the mention of Siberia for him to renounce his claim. Still, after that interview he was a poor Prince no longer. Our Secret Service Fund took care of that. It was nothifig very great they gave him, but sufficient to enable him to live com- fortably." "Were you authorised to offer him the throne of Greece?" asked the Vicar, inno- cently.

The ex-Ambassador smiled broadly. "No, but that did not _ matter. To make an offer and withdraw from it gracefully is diplo- macy," he gravely remarked, as he looked at his watch, reminded our hostess of his years, Nuci begged her permission to retire. ITER END .1 •

Bstablished 1828.

It is only necessary to ANOTHER compare the proposals just EDUCATION put forward by the Cougre- SCHEME. gational Union for a settle-

merit of the education problem with those put forward by Bishop KNOX a fortnight ago at the Church Congress to realise how wide is the gulf which separates the leaders of the two opposing schools of thought on this important question. Anything like a settlement by general agreement still seems as far off as ever, and it is much to be feared that, unless a more compromising spirit is displayed on both sides the country will be driven to the policy of secular schools in sheer despair. That the country should be in full possession of the views of both sides is, of course, all to the good, but no per- manent settlement will ever be reached by adopt- ing either view in its entirety. In 1902 Mr. BALFOUR completely ignored the existence of the Nonconformists. If they had been considered in the least much of the present trouble would have been avoided. But because Mr. BALFOUR was so unwise as to shut his eyes to all views except those of his own party is surely no reason why we should expect the Liberal Government to adopt similar tactics. We notice that even such a doughty fighter as Dr. AlassiE, who belongs to the " passive resister " school of thought, is con- strained to admit that denominationalism in education has its roots deep in the ground. It is all very well for Mr. flutsT HOLLOWELL and a few extremists to declare that they will not accept anything in the way of provision for denomina- tionalism of the nature of Clause 4 of Mr.BIRRELL's Bill. The extremists on the other side declare that they will not have anything in the nature of Clause 1 of Mr. BIRRELL'S Bill, and the violent opposition of these two sections to different parts of that bill was perhaps the best guarantee the moderate man in the country had that it was a fair and honest attempt to settle the problem without doing substantial injustice to either side. If the Liberal Government were to legislate in the same spirit as Mr. BALFOUR, it would only pro- voke reprisals just as the Act of 1902 did, and it is sincerely to be hoped that moderate counsels will in the end prevail, and that educationists may then be able t) devote the energy now expended in discussing religious problems to the real and abiding work of the schools. Speaking entirely from the educational point of view, and ignoring for the moment the question of religious teaching, we are sorry to note a growing disposition to suggest what is known as "contracting out" as a settlement. That means, without any doubt, allowing Voluntary Schools to go back to the pre-1902 position, to forego rates, and live on Government grants plit voluntary contributions. In other words it means going back to the days of the "intolerable strain" and gross inefficiency in the schools. We would seriously suggest that this is a very big price to pay for peace, and would fain hope that British statesmanship will fiud a better way. Besides, it would not really he a settlement, for it would mean the beginning of the trouble all over again. Once again get a Government in power friendly to denominational schools, and there would be irresistible pressure put upon them to give special grants to Voluntary Schools, and thus set up the dual system again. The Act of 1902 was grossly unjust to Nonconformists, but it did at least accomplish this, that the education of our children was made a communal obligation, and had no dependence on charity. This is a principle which ought not lightly to 1).3 set aside even to settle the religious diffi- culty. Let us fight our battles about the schools outside as vigorously as we wish, but during the fight let us all agree on all hands that the education of the children shell not suffer. They are no parties to the conflict, but surely they are the most important factor in the situation. If, we cannot agree about their religious education, surely that is no reason why we should let their general education suffer. The conflict outside the schools is interesting, and at times severe. It has already lasted, since the Voluntary Schools Act of 1897, throughout the whole school life of many children, and it may last thrciugh the school lives of many more. Really we ought to agree to keep them out of it, and not let their educa- tion suffer while we fight.

The question is now being AUSTRALIA AND debated by Australian THE NATIVE politicians whether it INDIAN. would not be advisable to permit the tropical portion of the vast island-continent to be colonised by British Indians. Too country is too vast to make it likely that white settlers will ever colonise it at all, even if the climatic difficulty had not to be taken into account. Although the Australian has strong race prejudices, he is beginning to recognise that it is better to have his country developed by Indians than to keep it empty for ever. The question at once arises as to the €ll.3ct of such a move on British India. It would almost

-47

appear that the opening up of a vast field for her surplus population would be welcome to over- populated Hindustan. On the other hand, we can- not fail to observe how slow is the movement of our own surplus population to the Colonies which are crying out for labour. The fact is that a new country has no use for any but the best citizens. The incompetent and the loafer will get on badly anywhere. The workers the Colonies want are well enough off in the Old Country. Does it not appear certain that the same considerations would apply with even greater force in India? How would our fellow-subjects in the sub-continent appreciate the influx of a few millions of the undesirables India could so well spare? The con- servatism of the East is another drawback, and, judging by the fact that the majority of Chinese emigrants belong to the poorest and least desirable sort, it would appear that this conservatism is strongest among the better-class Asiatics, and that it tends to keep them from leaving their native land. Presumably, however, the Australian authorities would only relax their strict alien immigration laws to permit the desirables to enter the country. In that case Australia might, without danger to herself, become a safety valve for India. Those carrying on the present agitation in Bengal are almost entirely educated young men, for whom there is no employ- ment of the kind their education has fltted'thern for. Give them an opening in a new country, and there is no reason why they should not prove good citizens.

In another column we pub- MR. CROSFIELD, M.P. lish an important letter from AND THE RAILWAY the Borough Member on the Crusis. railway crisis, in response to an appeal from a Warrington railway worker. Mr. C7osfield's reply is one that will give the utmost pleasure to not only the large body of railway employees in Warrington, but to the huge majority of his constituents who, we know, have the true interests of the workers at heart. It has sometimes been urged against Mr. Crosfield—quite unreasonably we think—that he is too much of a capitalist to ever adequately represent the workers in a constituency like Warrington. Nothing that Mr. Crosfield has ever done lends colour to the idea in the least, but some people are inher- ently suspicious of the man whom they think was born with a silver spoon in his mouth. Ever since he entered political life Mr. Crosfield's sympathies have been avowedly with the democracy, and since his elevation to Parlia- ment he has consistently supported the measures which made for better labour conditions. To-day he is found where all who knew him were sure he would be found in this desperate crisis—on the side of the workers and against the arbitrary dictates ,of capital. Mr. Croafield says plainly that the attitude of the Railway Directors is unpardon able and unpatriotic, and that they "seem to be a maple of hundred years behind the times." He argues strongly in favour of collective bargaining in organised industry, and his firm support to tl.ie men's claim for recognition will not only give satisfaction in Warrington, but will not be with- out effect elsewhere. We hope other M.P.'s will follow the straight lead of the Member for Warrington.

The member for Knutsford MR. KING, M.P. Division which extends

in one direction from Preston Brook to the Cat and Fidtile—is working hard to keep his constituents posted up in current politics. Between the 22nd inst and November 18th Mr. King will address no less than fourteen meetings in the division, one of which will be at Stretton on Thursday evening next. Mr. King is being supported on the plat- form by Mr. Fred . Horne (a brother of the Rev. Sylvester Horne) and by the end of the year he will be able to boast that he has addressed meet- icgs in every one of the twenty-five polling districts in the constituency. The Knutsford Division is one of the most difficult to work in the country, and the enthusiasm which actuates both Mr. King, M.P., and his indefatigable agent, Mr. J. Smith, is very commendable.

Our friend the "Observer " TORIES AND published on SAturday some SOCIALISM. opinions on Socialism from "leading Warringtoniaus," i.e., Mr. W. P. Rylands, Mr. F. Monks, Councillor Fulton-Smith, Aldeeman Bennett, and Mr. A. J. Jolley.We suppose our contemporary was emulating the" Standard" and sought adverse criticisms of Socialism, for the gentlemen appealed to are all— with the possible exception of Mr. F. Monks— deeply dyed with true Tory blue. Their views make amusing reading, and while all differ, none seem to be blessed with much understanding of the question except Alderman Bennett, whose candour has an honester ring than the remarks of the others. Mr. W. P. Rylands says " the aim and object of Socialism is to level mankind. To level up is impossible, and consequently this object can only be achieved by a process of levelling down." We wonder if Mr. Rylands expresses the views of his party when he says it is "impossible to level up." This is a sad confession for a politician with Parliamentary aspirations to make, and at the same time leads us to the conviction that the most reactionary type of Conservatism is all that is left for the "constitutional party" to espouse. Mr. F. Monks has a little more faith in the possibility of "levelling up," which is perhaps due to some lingering strain of Liberalism in his composition, but Councillor Fulton Smith would have Socialists dumped into Russia there to set up a colony "on the lines of liberty, equality, and fret srnity." We suppose poor old monopoly-ridden England is quite unsuited for anything so revolutionary as "liberty, equality and fraterity " ? Alderman Bennett sees much good in the Socialist ideal, but yet feels bound to combat it. His brilliant sugges tion is to frankly accept whatever is goo I in Socialism and "couple if: with Imperial Union and Tariff Reform." What a coupling! The progeny of such a union would assuredly prove too much even for so great a theorist as the author of "The Origin of Species." With their customary shortsightedness the Tories are booming Socialism, and sounding their own death-knell. The more thought the people give to Socialism and the more rapidly will they become convinced that, at any rate, there is no place in these democratic days for the purse- proud and privileged Tory party.

The town of Warrington is WARRINGTON SLUMS. generously provided with slum property—one of those legacies from the past which the present genera- tion finds a difficulty in disposing of. The town has also an abnormally high death-rate, especially among infants,and it has been openly asserted that one evil is a contributory cause of the other. A fortnight ago we commented upon an elective auditor's (Mr. E. England's) criticism of the state of things existing in the town, and welcomed so outspoken an opinion from such a quarter. Auditors of municipal accounts now-a-days are expected to go further than the mere certification of accurate book-keeping, and in fact we do not see that it matters much who the critic is so long as he has a stake in the town and expresses an honest desire to see some of its drawbacks removed. Mr. England's facts and figures created some stir, and we believe they have already borne good fruit. Mr. J. E Richardson, the new President of the Tradesmen's Association, alluded in his inaugural address to the amount of slum property in Warrington, and we note that a sensible list of questions addressed by the Association to candidates for municipal honours concludes with the following significant query: s.—Are you prepared to support an active policy having for its object the demolition of slum property and the remedying of insanitary conditions existing within certain areas in the borough? The opportunity for bringing about a great reform in the conditions of life among the poorer classes in Warrington lies ready to the hand of every member of, or aspirant to the Town Council. We hope the Health Committee will at once lead the way in a militant campaign against those con- gested areas which breed nought but disease and immorality.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Mr. W. Hall Walker, member for the Widnes Division of Lancashire, has voted against the nationalisation of railways in a plebiscite of the House of Commons taken by the " Financial Review of Reviews." "It would," he says, "be a financial fiasco, rendering no benefits to anyone except the pension- loving State Official."

Alderman Bennett —ever to the fore with an idea for the honour and glory of Warrington—suggegted at the Mayoral banquet on Thursday that Warrington should some day have a pageam more glorious and magnificent than the one one which we recently held at Liverpool ! Warrington had, he said, a history more illustrious than Liverpool. We should like to add "hear, hear." Warrington was a town of importance when Liverpool was only a pool in reality, and the pageant of time has brought some illustrious men and stirring scenes to our doors. « . *

"There is no law of God-, there ought to be no law of man, that so favours the man who owns property in land as to enable him to dispossess the people. . . . . . The rights of property concern a class, and are based on fulfilled duties, which concern the whole people. . . . The land was meant to serve the people's good whilst maintaining them. Without it the people cannot live, on it the people have a right to live, so it can become no man's absolute possession, to be done with as he wills."—Rev. A. M. Fairburn D.D. (" Religion in History and in Modern Life "21895.)

Though the opera in the Parr Hall has been quite a success this week, it has not aroused all the old, enthusiasm and the bookings have not been so prodigious as formerly. There was quite a wide- spread feeling that in re-producing the "Yeomen of the Guard" the Committee acted somewhat unwisely, for notwithstanding the popularity of the opera many people would have preferred to see the Musical Society's members in a new venture. Choice is not surely considered limited after only four years! We hope more enterprise will be shown next year. Either "Patience," "The Pirates of Penzance, or "Pinafore "would be likely to set the town in a " buz " of interest oncemore.. It seemed like being in a dream to hear falling from the lips of Mr. Henry Roberts at the Mayoral dinner the following sentence: "He was bound to say that he failed utterly to see in what sense or degree party politics had anything whatever to do with municipal administration." In his time Mr. Roberts has been as keen a party fighter at municipal elections as any man living. He was even credited with the author- ship of a party compact which reduced contested elections to a minimum, and in the words of critics, "disfranchised the ratepayers." Since retiring from the Town Council he has evidently seen the error of his ways. We are glad to find that in regard to politics in municipal affairs at least he ranges himseif alongside two other great men—Mr. Roosevelt and Mr. Dodds.

The meeting of the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress on Wednesday passed a reso- lution expressing entire sympathy with the railway- men, and their regret and surprise at the attitude of the directors. It is quite evident that the men will have the sympathy of the whole of the trade unions of the country and probably in some cases their active support. In the first week of November a meeting will he held of the Parliamentary Committee of the Trades Union Congress, the executive of the Federa- tion of the Trades Unions and the members of the Labour party. The gathering will, therefore, be representative in a very unusual degree of labour, and it will consider, among other things, the best way of helping the railwaymen in the fight for the recognition of their union.

* 0 The scholarship of the London County Council covers almost every career to which a boy may aspire. Three mercantile marine scholarships of £25 each have just been awarded for the first time. The parents are required to give an undertaking that the boys will "of their own free will" enter the mercantile marine service at the end of their course of training, which lasts for two years. The boys are sent first of all cu a voyage in a training ship to Australia. On their return they complete the period of their apprenticeship in sea-going ships. The scholarship money is paid to the Marine Society, to whose care the boys are committed.

- The last cottage has disappeared from the City of London. It was situated behind the National Bank, in Broad-street, and was a regular seventeenth century cottage, with wood and plaster front, a high-pitched roof of red tiles, and attic dormer windows. For long it has been occupied by a well-known firm of stock- brokers, who carried on their business in its low- roofed rooms. They were anxious to retain it, and offered to purchase the freehold, but the bajik required it for the extension of their premises, and so the old cottage had to go. It is said to be the oldest structure of the kind in the City, though in Holborn and else- where there are several buildings going much further back than the seventeenth century.

Notwithstanding the memorial signed by nearly every elected member of the Council the arbitration proceedings in London between Mr. Lawson and the Corporation were not open to the Press. At the open- ing of the arbitration on Monday Mr. Hill, the arbitrator, heard arguments from the Counsel engaged with regard to publicity, and Mr. Radcliffe, on behalf of Mr. Lawson, opposing the admission of the Press, the arbitrator sided with him. No one in Warrington was much surprised for the attempt to get the Press admitted, though so substantially backed,was regarded as a forlorn hope. It seems to be fated that the Lawson Water Contract shall remain a mystery to the end of the chapter, even though £70,000 of the ratepayers' money is involved. The Water Committee has muddled the whole thing ever since the extensions at Winwick and Delph-lane began. Alderman Pierpoint and Councillor Pemberton have represented the Investigation Committee at the arbitration and, possibly, they may have some information to give. * * * The act of a foolish man should never be a prece- dent.—TlLMUD. What does a woman want with logic? Instinct carries her through all illogicalities to that appearance of the truth which she loves.—E. TEMPLE THURSTON.

The secret of managing a man is to let him have his way in little things. He will change his plan of life when he won't change his bootmaker !—Mn-c. CRAiGIE.

• o Judging from what took place in the Wesleyan , Schoolroom, Padgate, last Monday, if one wishes to spend a pleasant and an amusing evening one could not very well do better than get up a debate formed on Parliamentary lines and introduce a Bill for "Women's Suffirage." Those who advocated the women's cause extolled the virtues of the ladies to a high degree and heaped scorn on the "mere man," putting the blame for the apparent slow progress in the world and social distress and discontent on his shoulders, much to the delight of the ladies present, especially when they heard in addition that they were quite the equal and in many cases superior to men. Those who spoke against" Women's Suffrage" ridiculed the latter proposition and talked about men's superiority, claiming that he was the lord of creation, whilst woman's place was at home, &c., &c. At the close of the debate the" House" was divided, the members going into the lobbies in charge of the " Whips." Singular to say despite the "ungallant ' remarks made about them the ladies voted against "Women's suffrage "and turned the scales against the men, quite overwhelming the kind intentions of those who pretended to be keen on giving them the vote.

New Church at Orford. With full Masonic ritual the laying of the corner stones of the Church of St. Margaret, Orford, took place yesterday afternoon in the presence of a large gathering including Freemasons from variodt parts of Lancashire. The Right Hon. the Earl of Lathom, P.G.W., R.W. Prov. Grand Master of West Lancashire, laid a stone to the memory of Mr. William Sharp and afterwards a memorial stone to the late Mrs. Beamont was laid by Mrs. Margaret Stapylton. The Price of Milk. A meeting of farmers and milk producers was held at the Blue Bell Inn, on Wednesday, to consider the wholesale price of milk. There was a very large attendance. Mr. Sadler, Crewe, had been announced to take the chair but he afterwards declined to do so, and Mr. Bridge consented to preside. Mr. Sadler then proposed that the price of milk be 2s. Gd.per dozen from the first of November to the last day in March. There was no seconder and the resolution that was finally adopted was" that the price of milk owing to the increased price of feeding staffs be five months at 2s. 6d. and seven months at 2s. pet' dozen. This resolution was passed on the motion of Mr. Longton seconded by Mr. Cooke. Progressive Labour League. What promises to be a very successful session of this League was opened on Monday evening when the large room at the Liberal Club was filled, on the occasion of a meeting called to arrange the winter Programme. Mr. J. Elwell (president of the League) presided and was supported by the treasurer (Mr. S. Wright, J.P.) and the secretary (Messrs. J. Morton and a• Howes, jun.) It was decided to hold meetings weekly, commencing next Tuesday evening. Several items of business were discussed and on the question of the municipal elections the opinion was expressed that the League should bring forward a candidate.
