Shorthand Writers' Association. The literary half of this Association's programme of last Wednesday's meeting, which was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, consisted of a lecture by the Rev, Canon Stevenson, M.A. The chair was taken by Councillor J. Fulton-Smith (one of the vice-presidents of the Association), a good num- ber of members and friends being present. The subject of the lecture was Tennyson's Gareth • and Lynette." The lecturer pointed out the many good ideals of the poem. In the" Coming of Arthur, which " Gareth and Lynette "follows, he said that the ideal was, to put it shortly,\ " to rid the kingdom of all that was wrong, to have power on this dead world to make it live, and to establish a true and righteous kingdom upon earth, to follow the Christ, the King ;" and in the idyll under notice was seen at itss very best—at what we may call the "golden aae."—A hearty vote of thanks to the Rev. Canon Stevenson for his interesting and instructive lecture, and also to Mr, Smith for his conduct in the chair, both of which were suitably acknowledged, terminated a very enjoyable and profitable meeting.

PETROLEUM OIL IN BARRELS and Drools delivered free. Ask prices.-1 S. GARLAND, iron- monger, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Express in Peril. On Monday evening the 5.30 express train from Manchester to Liverpool had a very alarming experi- ence. At a point between Irlam and Glazebrook, when the train was going at the usual express speed the attention of the engine-driver was attracted to some men on the line,vrho were frantically waving their arms. The engine was brought to a standstill in the shortest possible space, and only just in time to avert collision with two coal trucks, which by some mistake had just then been shunted on to the main line from sidings in the neighbourhood. But for the energetic signalling of the men and the ready action of the engine-driver, the consequences would in all probability have been very serious.—" A Grateful Passenger " writes : With reference to the above, I think very great credit , is due to the two men,Shaw and Longden, who averted what would appear to have been a terrible accident. , I therefore appeal to all passengers on this train, if not to all who travel upon the line, to join me in a subscription on behalf of these two men. The same may be forwarded to the following : —Mr. Kirk, statiou master, Central Station, Manchester ; Mr. Dening, station master, Central Station, Liverpool; and Mr. Daxbyshire, station master, Central Station, Warring- ton.

For PLAIN & SMOKED BACON try ASHHEY'S.—Advt. Warrington Philomathic Society. The second meeting of the above Society was held on Wednesday last, the President in the chair. The question, " Is Modern Civilization a Blessing ? " was discussed. Mr. G-. Pitt, opening in the affimative, endeavoured to prove that—even passing by the obvious advantages which were the outcome of modern civilization such as railways and other results of scientific research—the people were more. pure mentally, and consequently physically, than in any other civilized condition. The principle of modern civilization had been abused. The individual must see to it that he made full use of the opportunities which modern civilization offered to him. Mr. R. H. Mellor, opening in the negative, sighed for the "good old times." He thought it was a blessing when it took four days to get to London ; and when, during winter, one was often snowed up. He proved by statistics that lunatics were on the increase ; be pointed out the advantages of self-supporting States ; he thought that railway trains and tramcars should be abolished. In addition the enormous number of doctors proved that modern civilization was ruining ' the health of the country. A lengthy discussion, in ' which most of the members joined, ensued, and the question was carried in the affirmative by a large majority. WHITE CHEESE 6d.'rer lb., ASHLEY'S, Sankey St.—Advt. THE COMING MUNICIPAL ELECTIONS. Women's Opportunity.

Special interest attaches to the Municipal elections of 1907 from the fact that under the Bill passed at the instance of the Government in the past session women are now eligible for election as members of Borough and County Councils. It may be recalled that from 1870 onwards women, if possessed of the necessary qualifications, were able to sit on school boards, and in not a few instances rendered conspicuous service in this important branch of public work. Under the Act of 1902, however, they were deprived of direct ; representation on educational authorities, for the , local Councils took over the work of the School Board in England and Wales and women were not eligible to serve upon these bodies. But though they were thus excluded from the new authority, express pro- vision was made for their co-option on the education committees, and in this capacity they have continued to render valuable service. It , was scarcely to be expected, however, in these days of the active asser- tion of women's rights that they would be content with the subordinate position to which they were relegated by the Act of 1902 and the measure introduced in the late session received general support, the only point of difference arising on a proposal made in the House of Lords that women should not be eligible for the office of Mayor or the chairmanship of a County Council. Ultimately a compromise was agreed upon, the Lords filithdrawing their amendment on condition! that words were inserted providing that a ! woman Chairman or Mayor shall not become an ex-officio magistrate. Although, taking the countryl as a whole, the number of womea candidates at the pending elections is likely to be small, there will be a number of interesting contests, and it will probably be found that capable women who offer themselves for election will be regarded with considerable favour by ! the electorate. There is a field of usefulness open to them in endeavouring to make the education given to ' girls more practical, and in promoting improved social conditions. There are indeed many subjects within the four corners of municipal work in which women are specially interested, such for example a's- good housing, the regulation of the employment of children, the protection of child life, the supervision of the female side of lunatic asylums, the oversight of industrial schools, the treatment of women in inebriate homes, the inspecion of lodging houses, the supervision of midwives and baby farms, and the prevention of disease. Indeed, it is scarcely to much to say that in every matter affecting female and child life the co-operation of women Councillors will be a great advantage to our municipalities. But our object in calling attention to the coming elections is not specially to advocate the claims of women candidates, for after all character and personal • qualifications are of far greater importance than mere considerations of sex. It is an act of simple justice that women should not be debarred from election, but it will be for the electors to weigh the claims of all the candidates who may present themselves, and to elect those who are best fitted by their char- acter, position and experience to render efficient service upon the local body. It is scarcely necessary to point out that the work of the munici- palities continually grows in importance. As we have already shown, they are now responsible for the education in the elementary schools, as well as for local schemes of secondary education, and in the sphere of sanitation their duties become increasingly onerous. The very pressure of municipal duties is indeed, a serious danger, for it makes it more and more diffi- cult to find business men who are able and willing to devote the necessary time to the public work of their localities. And it is clear that if the best type of

Warrington Nominations. Although Thursday next is the latest day for the receipt of nominations at the Town Hall, not ranch interest is at present being taken in the forth?,oming municipal elections. The only new candidate in the field is Mr. Samuel Davies, baker, Wellington-street. who has been selected by the Conservatives to oppose Councillor Clegg in Howley Ward. It is rumoured that Mr. W. B. Roberts (C) will come forward in Bewsey Ward where Councillor Skelland again seeks the suffrages of the electors. The candidates nominated up-to-date are :—St. John's Ward, Councillor Fulton- Smith ; Orford Ward, Councillor Wilkinson ; St. Austin's Ward, Councillor Barton ; Howley Ward, Councillor Clegg ; Bewsey Ward, Councillor Skelland ; Town Hall Ward, Councillor Wilding and Mr. W. Rogers.

Tradesmen's Questions to Candidates.

The Warrington Tradesmen's Association is taking an active part in the forthcoming municipal elections, and has addressed the following questions to the candidates who come before the electorate on November Ist. :

I. Are you prepared to support a scheme for the widening of Warrington Bridge or the erection of a new bridge? 2. Are you in favour of the immediate erection of a public abattoir, and the refusal of all fresh applications for the erection of slaughter houses ? 3. Are you in favour of a reduction being made in the charges made by the Corporation to users of elec- tricity as an illuminant? 4. Are you prepared to vote in favour of the abolition of the charr,es made by the Corporation for the collec- tion of trade refuse?

_ 5. Are you preparArl to support an policy haying for its object the demolition of slum property and the remedying of insanitary conditions existing within certain areas in the borough?

===== 0002601_19071019_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE PRINCE OF EVANGELISTS."

Lecture by the Rev: E. Davidson.

A very welcome visit was paid to Bewsev-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday by the Rev. Edward Davidson, who will be remembered as having given an inspiring lead to local Wesleyans a year ago. In the morning he took for his text" Blessed are the pure in heart., for they shall see God," while in the evening his discourse was based on the parable of the man who went to the wedding banquet without the required wedding garment. On the following evening Mr. Davidson delivered his popular lecture, " Moody, the Prince of Evangelists." Mr. R. I. JENKS, of Penketh, presided, and said the title of the lecture reminded him of his first experience of Messrs. Moody and Sankey, on two occasions thirty- five years ago, when in London. He then made the acquaintance of the man who was afterwards known as Professor Henry Drummond. He spent all the time with him in conducting young men's meetings. At Mr. Moody's second visit to London he formed a series of meetings which were of great assistance to him. The lecturer also reminded him of the prince of lecturers, the Rev. Morley Punshon. Mr. Davidson then proceeded with his lecture, which was listened to with appreciative interest. He styled Moody as one of the two great men (Abraham Lincoln and Moody): that America had given to the world during the last 50 years. The lecturer traced the evangelist's stirring career in an illuminating way, and at the end a collection was taken in aid of church expenses. The Rev. Sidney Pitt moved a vote of thanks to the Chairman and lecturer, and it was heartily agreed to.

MOTORS AND ROAD MAINTENANCE.

The Clerk stated that the Ross Rural District Council had sent the following resolution for approval •' That this Council desires to bring to the notice of the Local Government Board that although the motor car traffic which comes from all parts of the United Kingdom increases daily over the main roads of the country, the owners of cars not belonging to the county, contribute nothing towards keeping the roads in repair. That, under these circumstances, arrangements should at once' be made by which a more even distribution of money received by the Inland Commissioners for motor licences should be givsn to the countries in proportion to the mileage and costs of the main roads which they have to maintain." The resolution was referred to the Highways Execu- tive Committee on the motion of Mr. Morris seconded by Mr. Linaker.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
RESIGNATION OF A NURSE,

Mr. Woodward said that a meeting of the Isolation Hospital Committee was held on Tuesday morning, and he had to report that Nurse Boyle had sent in her resignation. She was the newly-appointed nurse and she had resigned not because she wp.s not satisfied with the Hospital, but because there was illness at the nurse's home and she had been obliged to leave. The resignation was accepted with regret. FINANCE. _ _

Mr. Woodward reported that paymants had been made for maintenance to the amount of £77 lls. 11d., leaving £1,865 17s. 10d, in the hands of the treasurer. The report was adopted.

A meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held on Tuesday at Dutton Workhouse, Mr. T. Baster, in the chair. There were also present : Messrs. J. Hallows, S. ‘Voodward, AdmirAl Jones Parry, J. J. Jones, G. Pilling, C. Roylance, P. Booth, J. H. Davies, C. E. Linaker, J. Morris, A. Norman, J. Bolshaw, J. B. Chadwick and G. F. Ashton (clerk), T. Cooke, J. Pearson, and W. ===== 0002601_19071019_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
POOR LAW CONFERENCE.

The Clerk informed the Board that Messrs. Baxter Wright, Mynekyme and Pilling attended the North- Western Poor Law Conference at the Town Hall, Liver- pool. Mr. :former Fust (chairmin of the conference) dealt with the classification of indoor paupers and advocated an extention of the area of the administra- tion of committees for dealing with indoor poor so that classification might be economically and properly carried out in the Workhouse. _ _

A paper was read by 111 r. A.J. Jolley of the Warrington Union on the best system of tenders and contracts. Administration was dealt with more than the general principle. The delegates visited the training ship Indefatigable " to which Boards !of Guardians send children to be trained for the navy or mercantile marine. The ship appeared to be well managed and the best of training seemed to be given to the boys. A paper was read on the combination of Unions for the treatment of vagrants by Mr. Dean clerk to the TJlverston Union. He strongly advocated the combination of the Unions of Lancashire, Cheshire, Cumberland and Westmorland, for dealing with casual paupers so that there might be a uniform system with the object of stamping out vagrancy. A list was read showing the number of casual paupers relieved for the year ending July, 1907, compared with the previous year ; 1906, males 1935' females 187, chil- dren 20, total 2,142 •, 1907, males 1,770, females 207, children 19, total 1,996. The conference resolved that a combination of Unions was the best method of dealing with the vagrant class. The Chairman, in proposing the adoption of the report, said he attended the meeting, and it was a very interesting visit throughout. He had great pleasure in proposing the adoption of the report. Mr. Dilynekyme seconded, and the report was adopted unanimously.

TIER COST OF PAUPERISM.

Mr. Dansey, Local Crovernmeat Board Inspector, presented his annual statement of pauperism and expenditure, which showed that the acreage of Runcorn Union was 50,434 acres, and the population according to the last census 39,561 and the rateable value £257,686. Tbe number of paupers on the Ist January, 1907, was 742, of which 178 were in-door and 567 outdoor. The expenditure on indoor was £983 out-door £1,575, total £2,558; a decrease of £5B. The cost per head of the population was is. 3d. There were only two Unions in Cheshire with a less percentage of that amount, namely, Bncklow, 11d. ; Tarvin, is. lid. ; and Wirral!, is. 2id. The highest percentage was Chester 2s. 21d. A LADY'S PROTEST.

The following letter was read by the clerk : "Hatton Grange, near Runcorn, October Ist, 1907. Dear Mr. Ashton. Please convey my thanks to the Guardians for putting my name on the Ladies Visiting Committee to the Workhouse. I retired from the committee three years ago, partly on grounds of ill-health partly because I thought it was not for the best interests of the House for us always to be accom- panied by an official when on our visits of inspection. I know of other ladies' visiting committees who are not under such rules and who arrange for their meetings whenever they please, without the officers knowing when they are coming or even seeing the ladies unles asked for. Unless these restrictions have been done away with, will you kindly withdraw my name from the committee. With kind regards, your truly, LA AI ALE BOSTON."

The resignation was accepted with regret. FINANCE.

Mr. Woodward produced a report of the Finance Committee which stated that payments had been made to the amount of £1,484 14s. id.. leaving a balance in the treasurer's hands to the amount of £4,107 13s. 9d. The report was adopted.

ST. HELENS.

"THE MARTYRS" AT ST. HELENS.

The Assembly Room at the Si. Helens Town Hall was packed on Tuesday night with a most -enthusiastic audience on the occasion of the first subscription con- cnrt of the season of the St. Helens Glee Club. The first half of the concert was given over to a most successful performance of Maunder's dramatic cantata for male voices " The Martyrs." The chorus and orchestra numbered over 140, and the principals were Master Joseph Green, of St. Mary's, Sheffield; Mr. Charles Tree, baritone; and Mr. J. E. Walmsley, tenor. Under the capable direction of Dr. S. B. Siddall, the founder of the club, the work was rendered in most striking style, the effect of the great triumphal chorus being most remarkable. Mr. J. H. Maunder, the com- poser of the oratorio, was present,aad at the conclusion congratulated Dr. Siddall most heartily on the magnifi- cent performance, saying that the work had never been better rendered.—The second part of the programme was of a miscellaneous description. One of the finest items was the vocal quintet "The Hero's Rest," sung by Miss Bessie White,Dr. Siddall, Messrs. R. Lloyd Moore, J. Gilroy and E. Baylis. The orchestra was under Mr. Gordon E. Stutely, Liverpool.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
SEQUEL TO A RAINEIILL CYCLE ACCIDENT.

At St. Helens, on Wednesday, Mr. H. Joplin, of Huyton, was sued by Mrs. Mary Crompton, widow, of The Holt, Rainhill, for personal injuries caused by being run down by defendant's bicycle.—Mr. Wright, for the plaintiff, said that on Friday, May 17, about ton oclock at night she was crossing in front of her own house when she was knocked down by a bicycle and seriously hurt. It was very dark and raining at the time, and defendant was showing no light.—Mrs. Crompton said she was confined to bed for three weeks,and complainant could not walk properly even yet.—Defendant said about four or live yards from where complainant was knocked over there was a strong beam of light from a shop window. This prevented himfrom seeing anyone.—Mr. Segar argued that plaintiff was guilty of negligence in , not looking along the road. It had been proved that defendant's lamp was lit.—Judge Shand found that the accident was attributable to the negligence of the defendant. It was no doubt a very awkward thing, both in riding a bicycle and in driving, to come to a place where there was a glaring incandescent light in one's eyes, but if they did they must not go on and chance it. They must take adequate precauti3ns and not go and run into things. Defendant might have had his light burning, but he did not ring his bell. He believed that defendant was going a good deal faster than he admitted, and he gave judgment for plaintiff for twelve guineas with costs. ST. HELENS CORPORATION EXHIBITIONS.

At a meeting of the St. Helens Parks Committee, on Wednesday, Mr. A. Lancaster (borough librarian) submitted a most satisfactory report on the recent exhibition at the Victoria Park. This is the first time 'an educational exhibition of the kind had been held in the town and the exhibits of work done in the Elemen- tary and Technical schools attracted a great amount of attention. Visits had been paid by persons from Liver- pool, Southport, Wigan, and other places, and a deputa- tion from the Education Committee, Westhoughton, made a special inspection. The exhibition had been open 90 days, and the attendance had been very satisfactory, the total number of visits paid being 17,620. The nominal charge of one penny for admission had produced £45 ss. Bd. and the ordinary expenses had been £47. The previous exhibition in 1900 cost over £4OO. and the receipts were £96 10s. The exhibition just closed had appealed to and delighted all classes in the town. Mr. Lancaster also reported that the Board of Education, South Kensington Museum had sanctioned the loan to the St. Helens Corporation of the first historical collection of water-colour paintings, together with a collection of objects of industrial art for exhibi- tion at the Victoria Park next summer. The exhibits would fill four or five of the largest rooms in the park mansion.

ST. HELENS RATES.

Mr. John Brown, the borough treasurer of St. Helens on Monday, issued his annual rep3rt and statement of accounts of the Corporation. Out of a total rate, including poor rate, of ss. 6d. in the pound, no less than 11.06 d in the pound was spent on elementary education as well as a new charge of id. on Cowley Middle Schools against last year's rate of 8.20 d. The capital expendi- ture on new schools up to the close of the last financial year was £19,414, but this will probably be more than doubled during the current year. The liabilities, including the working balance,are £35,987. The Distress Committee's accounts show that £6O ss. was spent in providing employment for men out of work, but the results of their, labour were sold for £37 15s. The Corporation have a nest egg of £1,899 in three relief funds, which have been in existence some years. lOn the Transvaal War Fund the amount in the bank is £1,283, on the Imperial Yeomanry Equipment Fund there is £5lO, and on the Local Relief Fund there is £lO5. The rateable value of the borough on the high- way account is £375,693, which is the highest on record. The auditors report that on the gas works account the excess of assets over liabilities is £105,174 165., an increase for the year of £2,635. The profits on the gas works were £8,218 I3s. 9d., which was disposed of as follows :—£5,000 was appropriated to the borough fund in reduction of rates; £2,761 to capital account, and £456 17s. in part payment of improvements. The total amount of profits carried to the borough fund since the purchase of the works is £76,300, although the price of gas has during that time been reduced from 4s. to 2s. 2d. per 1,000 feet. On tha electricity account there is a profit, and the excess of assets has increased by £5,271. On the tram- ways capital account the increase is £1,903, but on the year there was a Joss of £1,195 for maintenance of track, &c. On the waterworks account there was a small profit, but the assets show an increase of £4,234, and now stand at £118,235. The capital expenditure on pumping stations, reservoirs, &c., amounts to £444,303. The capital expended on the Highways and Borough Fund account amounts to £411,505. During the year £6,030 of stock has been redeemed. the net result being that the excess of assets has increased by £11,201 on the year. The outstanding debt on tie six main ' accounts—gas, water, general, electricity, tramways, and education—amounts to £872,321. There is at pre- sent in sinking and stock redemption funds, set aside in accordanee with the requirements of the Local Government Board, the sum of £56,392, leaving a net debt of £815,929.

EARLESIOWN AND ritAITON

THE SOCIALIST HALL.

The new Socialist Hall, in Wargrave-road, Earles- town, was opened last week, and already the Society has enrolled over 100 members. It is intended to hold debates on Thursdays and Saturdays, and during the winter musical at hpmes," social evenings, 6:c. The National Administrative Council of the I.L.E'. with which this Society is afllliated,has since 1893 carried on a great propaganda work throughout the country, having spent over £250,000 on this work, and at the present time over 2,000 meetings per week are being carried on under its auspices, while the Literature .:ep,trtment has doubled its output within the last few months.

NEW CURATE AT EARLESTOWN.

The new curate appointed to Earlestown is the Rev, Samuel Devinney, L.L.D., of Trinity College, Dnblin. He was ordained deacon in 1875 and priest in the following year by the Bishop of IMmore. He has held cnracies in Ireland at Temploport, co. Cavan, 1875-6; Clones, co. Monaghan, 1878.80; vicar of Lack or Claglaty, co. Fermanagh, 1880-3 ; curate of St. James, West Bromwich, 188.3-8 ; St. Catherine, Wigan, 1888.91; St. Martin, Liverpool, 1891-9 ; St. Cleopas, Toxteth Park, Liverpool, 1899-1900; St. Philip, Liverpool, 1900-4; St. Silas, Toxteth, from 1905. lie resides at 81, Queen's-road, Liverpool. He is the author of' Law of pleading and evidence," "Common and Criminal law" and " Political Economy."

MORE MOTOR CAR CASES.

At the Newton Petty Session last Thursday William Arthur Wu-de was summoned for having driven a motor car at excessive speed on August 25th, at Newton. He dinitted the offence.—!r. !Cnowles, prosecuting, said the defendant's speed worked out at 29 miles an hour, he having been timed by the police between Hulme Bar and the Reformatory. He did no harm, but he might have done a good deal, many people being about the road that Sunday afternoon.—Police-Serc,beants Bannister and Clark, who did the timing—the former at the Reformatory and the latter at Hulme Bar—said they estimated the speed at 30 miles an hour, or nearly, and found that it worked out at 29 miles. Chere was traffic on the road, and it was dangerous.—Fined £2 and costs. George A. A. Bennett, of Coventry, was summoned for driving a motor car at a dangerous speed, and for refusing to stop at a police officer's request.—Ou the first charge Mr. Knowles said this was ou August 29th. A little before 2 p.m. the defendant came round the corner near the Legn Arms,Newton,at a speed estimated at 25 miles an hour—such a speed that the driver could not keep close to the corner in turning it, and if there had been anything in the way it was very likely he would have been unable to avoid a collision. Going down Church-street the car passed two men near the church at a speed which they thought 30 miles an hour. Had there been anything coming out of Willow-road no one could tell what the consequences might have been. There was accident after accident on this county main road, and it was sometimes difficult to bring the offence home, but there was no doubt in tut. case.—P. C. Wooff said he was with Inspector limes near the railway arches at Newton when the car came round the corner at sue') a rate that the back swung round, and witness thought the car must have been going 25 miles an hour. There were five children in sight on the road. —Defendant r.aid it would be an absolute mechanical impossibility for the car to go round the corner at the rate described without overturning. His car was a noisy one bat his speed was not what was alleged.— Fined £5 and costa. On the second charge of not stopping when required by the Police Inspector, defendant said he did not remember being required to stop.—Fined £3 and cost Woods' Great Peppermint Care, for Coughs and Colds; never fails. ===== 0002601_19071019_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves.

-Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Ezaminer " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each. week.

111 A contemporary is following the example of the "Standard" and is attacking Socialism. It tells us that " Socialism is an attack upon the stability and organization of society; is an attack upon the enjoy- ment by any man, high or low, rich or poor, of the fruit of his labours - is an attempt to impose upon the conscience, the goods, and the liberty of every one of us as an intolerable and grinding tyranny." It has invited several leading Warringtonians to give a summary of their views on Socialism, and their replies make interesting reading. Mr. W. PETER RTIANDS states: "Admittedly the aim and object of Socialism is to level mankind. TO level up is impossible, and consequently this object can only be achieved by a process of levelling down." Is there any justification for such a statement? Mr. F. MONKS states, in the course of his letter : "If, however, we mean by Socialism the growth and development of , those ideals which lift Society to a higher level of thought and action, making it better, healthier, and more progressive in ways not possible by individual effort, then what objection can there be to such a levelling up?" How conflicting are their views ! How contradictory! Socialists aspire to no dead level. They seek no reduction to a uniform level of physical measurement, of height, chest capacity, or muscular vigour. They do not expect that all our intellectual powers shall be of the same order in kind or degree. They admit the immutability of the law of natural selection as of all other natural laws.

Mn. COUNCILLOR J. FULTON SMITH would consign all Socialist leaders and their followers to " Australia, Canada, or Russia for choice." He will tell us, probably, whether or not the Earth is the birthright of all? It would also be interesting to know how he would set about solving the housing problem and unemployment he refers to; and what he believes to be the cause of so much misery and slumdom ? MR. A. J. JOLLEY philosophizes. Mr. Jolley will perhaps tell us whether or not it is a fact that in 'Societies, no less than in races of animals, those which cannot change must perish; whether the change of social structures in the direction of greater adaptation is not social progress; and whether periods of stagnation are not followed by what are called reforms or even revolutions? Ernest Haeckel tell us

that: "If a man desire to have the advantage of living in an organised community, he has to consult not only his own fortune, but also that of the Society, and of the 'neighbours' who form that Society. He must realize that its prosperity is his own prosperity, and that it cannot suffer without his own injury.' ALDEMIIN BENNETT adopts a reasonable tone of argument. He confesses that he is at a less to under- stand why, " if it is intrinsically right and reasonable for the community to make its own gas and supply its own water, it should be intrinsically wrong and foolish for it to supply its own milk, for instAnce, or to make its own soap; or why if it is good for the municipality to own its own trams, it should be bad for the State to own its railways." Mr. Aldermsn Bennett is a man for whom we have the greatest respect. We would ask him whether he thinks that the Tory party to which he belongs agrees with his views on these great questions. _ _ _

The Junior Conservative.; held a debate on Monday evening on " Conservatiyesna and Labour: Are the Conservatives opposed to the aims and objects of Labour ? " Mr. Broadhurst stated with regard to old age pensions that if a practical scheme was brought forward the Conservative party would heartily support it. They evidently intend that another party shall bring it forward. Fancy the Tory party supporting Old Age Pensions! What next? Mr. G. N. VERNON contradicted a statement by Mr. Taylor that many Church of England clergymen and Roman Catholic priests professed Socialism. He said there were few, if any. Mr. G. N.Vernon is not of an inquiring turn of mind. evidently. Mr. Donn believed in ChriAtian. Socialists but not in pol;timl Socialists That statement is so ambiguous that it takes a Junior Conservative atone to under- stand it.

Mr. CIMPIES said that it was useless to criticize the programme of the Labour Party for the simple reason that it had none. How really interesting and how edifying the debates of the Junior Conservatives must ! No programme! If he will call at Friars' Green House I think one w;11 be found for him. My dear fellow, the Tories and Liberals are tumbling over themselves in their anxiety to copy from it. JOHN WORKAA.N.

CHRISTIANITY AND SOCIALISM. CHRISTIAN SOCIALISNI NOT MODERN SOCIALISM.

I John Workman begins his last letter by saying that 1 I am" beginning to grumble because my friends have not backed me up in my argument." I" have before complained of misrepresentation and do so once more. It is tiresome to argue with a controvertialist who per- sistently and deliberately mis-states his opponent's position, and for this reason it is questionable whether I should continue this debate and so give John Workman more words to twist out of their meaning. I complained that the column purporting to be the "Vice of Labour" was almost entirely taken up by Socialist plans for the renovation of Society and little or nothing found a place voicing the opinions and aspirations of 'Labour. John Workman misrepresents this as above. I have done no grumbling about not being backed up. I have neither sought nor needed help. John Workman asked why the members of certain Associa- tions do not send their contributions. "Is it because they are afraid of defending the present competitive system &c.?" This proves my point no to the hilt. These members if they were to write would be expected not to write about the opinions and aspirations of labour but in defence of individualism against Socialism and the •Socialist Editor and his colleagues would be on the spot to answer. Is any str9nger proof wanted to show that the column ought to have its proper name, viz., The "Voice of Socialism ?" The present title has the hands of Esau but the contents speak in the tones of Jacob. The trick is as old as the days of the Patriarchs. John Workman can call his column what he likes but the present name is a Agnomen The Warrington Volunteers would have as much right to call them- selves the British Army and the little torpedo boat to think itself the Royal Navy. John Workman quoted a remark of mine about the sympathy existing between Socialists in different countries, and because of this asks: "Does not Socialism stand for the workers according to his own showing ?" No! Certainly not, only for a section. Then there follows a very lengthy quotation from Judge Hughes' preface to Maurice's tract in which olnnta,ry Associations corresponding to present day vo-orerative Societies which give a bonus to labour Cie mentioned and advocated. What this has to do wth the compulsory State Socialism advocated by John Workman it is difficult to say. The design of these Associations was to give each man the fruit of his labour after paying interest on the capital provided by the workers and others. They were based on the Biblical axiom contained in a quotation I have already given from Kingsley in describing the objects of the Christian Socialists "rewarding every man according to his works." One would have thought that the principle of reward as laid down by Jesus Christ would have had some weight with John Workman and he would have hesitated before trying to improve upon it. 1 But if anything more is needed to prove my point that the Socialism of the Christian Socialists, like IMaurice and Kingsley, was entirely different, let me quote the opinion of one of the ablest of Socialist . writers, Mr. Sydney Webb. In his " Socialism True ! and False" he classes the Socialism of Owen and j Louis Blanc, two of the names Maurice mentions as 1 follows "If we reject Owen's Trade Socialism as a 1 spurious form of collectiveism, certain to develop I into joint stock individualism on a small scale? 1 The zealous and devoted men who made the 1 Christian Socialist Movement of 1848-54 and who got their ideas from Louis Blanc and the Paris I Socialists of 1848, sought to replace the Capitalist entrepreneur not by the officials of the community hut by little groups of independent workmen jointly owning the instruments of their trade and co-oper- ating in a self-governing workshop." And in speaking I• of this plan he says" from beginning to end it is 1 diametrieally opposed to the Socialist ideal." ' I do not therefore retract, but repeat that John IWorkman shewed carelessness in trying to use the authority of Maurice and Kingsley in support of

the Socialism which he advocates when he knew, or ought to have known, that the Christian Socialism of these men was "something entirely different" and in the words of Sydney Webb was "from beginning to end diametrically opposed to the Socialist ideal." John Workman, who abounds in quotations from other people's writings and fills four-fifths of his last letter with them, gives one from Mr. Sydney Oliver. May I say that while I am ready to answer anything John Workman brings forward I must decline to deal with everything written on Socialism. The quotation in question is "No one can pretend to claim the fruits of his own labour for his whole ability and opportunity for work- ing are plainly a vast inheritance and contribution of which he is but a transient beneficiary and steward." I acknowledge the fact of this but not the con- clusion drawn from it. It is only a partial presentation of the case. It is only in half truth and as such if pushed too far and without refernce to the other Ihalf becomes false. Every man is a steward, or rather a tenant, with a life interest, and as such is responsible for the profitable develop- ment of the estate during his charge of it. Any system of society which takes from the tenant the , natural reward of faithfulness and energy and gives lit those who may be unfaithful and lazy, undermines the principle of equity upon which all human affairs must be based, or red ruin will be the result. This would be the outcome of the system advocated by John Workman of giving to each according to his need without any reference to his faithfulness or energy. The proposed system is dishonest and it does not make it less so when the act of dishonesty is done in the name of the State. I mean in so far as it takes from the individual the result of his labour and gives no proportionate equivalent. I have no desire to be offensive. I am here speaking of the proposed system, not of the men who make it. Paris, October Bth, 1907.

CHARLES E. PARKER.

THE PAINTING OF LA.1113-POSTS,

j Sir,—ln the Town Council report I see Councillor Ball asks a question relating to the painting of gas standards. Alderman Tinnion on behalf of the Gas Committee says the standards are painted by the men (lamp-lighters) in the summer months or the staff would have to be reduced. I should like to ask Alder- man Tinnion, how it is the staff has not been reduced in the summer months when the gas standards have not been painted, for they are not painted every year?

The fact is my dear Alderman we have to neglect our own work to do this kind of work. What the Gas Committee want is cheap labour so that they can make big profits. _ _

Could toll you some tales that would turn your hair green about the way the Gas Committee treat their employees. But this will do some other time.— Yours truly,

LAMP POST.

RAILWAY RESPONSIBILITY,

The mind of Labour just now is chiefly occupied with the progress of the struggle for recognition by the railway Men's trade union. In view of this the reply of the Railway Companies Association has received the necessary prominet ce in the public Press. The pros and cons. of the dispute are freely stated on every hand, so that it is hardly worth while repeating them here. I would rather draw attention to the last paragraph of the Railway Companies Association's letter which read as follows: —" In the opinion of my council it is necessarily a matter'which those to whom the public and the shareholders look for the safe and efficient conduct of the railway service of the country must take the full responsibility of forming and acting upon their own conclusions." I could hardly have written a vaguer or more ponderous sentence myself, but if it means anything it means that the Directors are summed to be respon.- sible for the safe and efficient conduct of the railway service. Are we justified therefore in assuming that the directors are to be held responsible for the horrible stnash at Shrewsbury? And will the share- holders look to the Directors to pay the inevitable damages?

Wliat rot! The very questions would bring a smile to the face of that yellow monkey I once possessed which climbed a purple stick. Is it not already being said that the driver of the train was running his engine at excessive speed. If the lack of safety in this particular instance was the driver's fault and the blame be his, then the credit also for multi- tudes'of journeys safely performed should be his, and his confreres the signalmen, stoker, and guards. If so what becomes of the directors' safe and efficient conduct of the railway service? If the driver made a mistake he has paid the penalty with his life "Ha fears no more the heat of the sun Nor the furious winter's rages; He his worldly task has done, Home has gone and ta'en his wages." Assuming for the sake of the argument that this proved, skilful, reliable driver was to blame, then it would seem the safety and efficiency of the railway service are endangered by the present action of the Railway Directors, which means the substitution of less experienced men in the various departments of railway work in place of those likely to come out on strike. The fact is that if all Directors and Share- holders died to-morrow hardly one train in the country would fail to run schedule time the day after. But if the same number of workers died !

Joint stock enterprise (limited companies) has done a great deal to develop manufacture and com- merce, production and exchange, but it has rendered almost complete the cleavage between ownership and labour. Every element of earning disappears from profits when all those who resume the responsi- bilities of administration, of daily management, of technical and commercial direction, of financial con- trol are paid by fixed salaries. When the holder of capital was at the same time leader and manager of the enterprise the exploitation of man by man was hidden by a veil, but the shareholder as such is nothing but an investor of money, more or less clever, more or less fortunate. In the words of Vandervilde "like the legendary dragon couched on a bed of Rhine gold in its deep cavern, passive capitalism lies hugging its wealth in distant cities, motionless and formidable, scornful of the revolts and careless of the miseries of an unknown proletariat. I recline and possess Let me sleep !

LA.ZARUS.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The patrons of this increasingly pupular place of amusement have keen delighted with the programme submitted this week which has included seme very novel features. For the first time in Warrington Nfaurin's wonderful fountains are exhibited, 20,000 gallons of water being thrown on to the stage. With the aid of many coloured lights some exceedingly beautiful prismatic effects are obtained, and the scone is one of dazzling grandeur. The spectacle concludes with a representation of "The Water Nymphs," two living statues standing in the centre of a large fountain with water playing round in various forms. A splendid turn is provided by Captain Taylor's edu- cated baboons wnich go through some really wonderful tricks, including jumping, tumbling, tight-rope walking and balancing. Their intelligence is almost human and the display is about the best of its kind we have seen. Les Bollingers, the original rag painters, of Paris, give a unique entertainment, produciug some pretty pictures by means of an assort- ment of rags. Cullen and earthy and Little Cluley are highly popular comedians, and Willie Gardner is seen in a clever act on ice and roller skates. Two sketches. "An officers's servant" by the Brothers Adderley, and "House Full" by Damerell, Rutland and Company, create a deal of amusement. Raymond's Rio-Tableaux shows an excellent series of up-to-date pictures. NEXT WEEirS PROGRAMME. Another capital programme is announced for next week. Rapoli, whe is described as the world's greatest artistic and sensational juggler, heads the bill. PITBLIC BENEFIT.—TINDAR TAX CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASTIIRIL—Advt. Death of an Old Warrington Rechabite. The death of Bro. Thomas Gandy took place at his residence, Cairo-street, on Monday at the advanced nue of Si years. He was the oldest Rechabite in the Warrington District, having joined Charity tent No. 11 in the month of September 1842, just over 65 years ago. He had not taken any active part in the work of the tent. for some years on account of failing health hut he was always delighted when he saw or read of the progress and advancement of the Order. The late Bro. Gandy took an active part and occupied several important offices in the tent in the early part of his membership, and lip to the last, he always took a pleasure in calling to remembrance the happy associations connected with the work of the early pioneers of the Rechabite and Temperance movement in Warrington District It can be truly said of him : If T have made one weary life the brighter, If I have eased another's toil and pain, If I have made some comrade's burden lighter, I have not lived in vain. In February, 1887, Mr. Gandy retired from his position as foreman mechanic and chief engineer under the firm of Armitage and Rigby, Ltd., when he was presented with a handsome timepiece in recognition of the high esteem in which he was held after 36 years service. He was one of the oldest members of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers which be joined in 1843 and in recognition of his long services the Execu- tive Council of the £S.E. presented him with a special award of merit, at a public meeting in November,l9o2. Colour Photography. The centre of interest of the amateur as well as the professional photographer has recently been shifted to the possibilities of delineating nature in that efful- gence of colour splendour which has so long evaded him. The recent achievements of Messrs. Lumiere in the production of a plate sensitive alike to all the colours of the spectrum, enables the 'photogragher to realize an ideal Thich has long been thought impossible of attainment—that is the productiog of a coloured Photograph by direct action of the light in the camera itself. Although a wide publicity has been given in the photographic journals and in the newspapers to the work of the French experts above mentioned it has not been possible to give an ocular demonstration equally widespread of the results of their labours. Lovers of the art of the camera will, therefore be interested to learn that a specimen of the new colour photograph is now to be seen at the establishment of 11r. liar .1d Smith, photographic chemist, Bridge- street, where also the Autochrome Plate, as Messrs. Lumiere's new product is called, is ' to be had at an outlay well within the means of the average amateur' " HERALD " KITCHEN RANGES and " Peveril' , Fire Grates are unsnrnassed. The second prize, electro-plated spirit flicks, was won by Horwich ; and the third prize, alectro-plated inkstands, were taken by Rochdale. The pri7es were afterwards presented by Captain Crosfield, who congratulated the men on their neat work and smart appearance. Deputy-Commissioner, Colonel Trimble, C.M.G.. L.R.C.P. (Preston) in moving a vote of thanks to Captain Crosfield, alluded to the difficulty of sustaining interest, in ambulance work, yet he considered that the North Western district bad now a finer set of men than ever before. The No. 4. North-Western district, comprises Cheshire, Lancashire, WestmorelanJ, Cumberland, Isle of Man and Ireland. Subjoined are the names of the teams and the percentage of marks obtained : —Crompton 84 per cent., Horwich 82 per cent., Rochdale 29 per cent., Accrington 741 per cent., Burnley 73 per cent.. Northwich 60 per cent., Oldham 591 per cent., Hindle), 561 per cent., Lancaster 36 per cent, Workingmen's Mission. Bank Qnay. The annual meeting in connection with the Women's Bible Class of this mission was held on Mondsy. About, 150 members and friends sat down to tea in the school- room, after which a concert was given. Mrs. Oros- field (president of the class) presided, and was accom- panied on the platform by Mrs. Roome. Songs were rendered by Mrs Taylor, Mrs. Rowlands, Mrs. Tonge, Mrs. Thomas, Mrs. Williams, Mrs. Swarbrick, and Mrs. Cox; recitatims were given by Mrs. Highley,Mrs. Freeman and Mrs. Edwards and a reading by Mrs. Lawson.—During the evening the Secretary read the annual report,which showed that the class (which was opened in October, 1905) had a membership of 169, with an average attendance every Sunday after- noon of over 70. The sick visitors had paid 90 visits during the year, and the absentee visitors 153 visits. The report paid testimony to the splendid services tendered to the class by the leader, Mrs. Moore. After the reading of the report, Mrs. Crosfield, on behalf of the members of the class, presented to Mrs. Moore a beautiful silver tea service and silver mounted oak tray, which had been sub-! scribed for by the members of the class. Mrs. Cros- field, in making the presentation, expressed the great pleasure it gave her to be there to band the gift toi Mrs. Moore, as she realised how very much the success ; of the class was due to that lady's leadership. The presentation was a light token of the loving esteem in which the members regarded their teacher. She (Mrs. Crosfield) sincerely hoped that Mrs. Moore would long be spared to continue the good work in which she was engaged. Mrs. Moore (who was taken by surprise, having no previous knowledge of the presentation) sincerely thanked the members for their gift, which she very much appreciated because of the kindly feeling it conveyed. The meeting was a very *successful one, thanks to the arrangements made by the Secretary and Assistant Secretary (Mrs. Hackney and Mrs. J. Hassall) and the Treasurer (Mrs. T. Taylor).

LAWSON CONTRACT.

THE ARBITRATION IN LONDON. Press Refused Admission.

The arbitration proceedings respecting the claim or Mr. George Lawson, contractor, aeainst the Warring- ton Corporatin. were opened on Monday morning at the Surveyor's Institute. London.

The Arbitrator uofIS Mr. Genre Henry Hill, M For the plaintiffs were lir. Radcliffe ICC., and Mr. G. A. Scott, and for the Corporation Mr. A. A. Hudson and Mr. Inman.

Before the proceedings were opened the Arbitrator stated that he bad received a memorial from one side asking that the Press might be admitted to the hearing. Mr. Hudson said his clients were not signatories to the memorial as a body, but he desired to say that they had not the slightest objectioo to the admission of the Press. It was within the province of the arbitrator to decide in the matter. The Corporation had no desire to cover anything or keep anything back.

Mr. Radcliffe said his clients wished to keep the enquiry confined strictly to business. The points at issue depended on the engineering interpretation of the contract. He did not wish the enquiry to be turned into a place for the airing of grievances. The arbitrator was appointed by the Courts RR a private arbitrator and in the ordinary way, and it was quite nnusuol to ftdmit the Press unless both patties were agreed His clients did not agree. Mr. Hudson said he accepted the terms of the appointment of the arbitrator as interpreted by his friend.

The arbitrator stated that being so it was not desir- able the Press sbonld be present and the reporters were asked to withdraw.

THE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

PRESENFATION AT WINDSOR.

The "Windsor and Eton Express" of Saturday last contains an account of a presentation to Mr. Martin Nicholls, Chief Constable of Warrington, who prior to taking up his appointment here occupied a similar posi- tion in the Royal Borough : A large and influential company foregathered in the Reception Room of Windsor Town Hall on Thursday afternoon, when an interesting presentation was made to Chief Constable Nicholls on his promotion to Warrington. The respect and esteem in which Mr. Nicholls is held was evidenced not only by the gifts, but by the cordial reception which was given him on all hands. The Mayor of Windsor (Alderman J. Brown) presided, and among those present were, besides Chief Constable Nicholls, Alderman E. Bampfylde, J.P., the Mayor-Elect, Councillor Sir William Shipley, J.P., and Lady Shipley, Sir John Soundy, J.P., Councillors J. E. Mitchell, J. Bedborough. C. F. Dyson, W. Carter, E. Atkins, ind W. H. Jackson the Town Clerk (Mr. E. C. Durant), the Magistrates' Clerk (Mr. A. Lovegrove), Lieut. and Quartermaster Harford (Royal Horse Guards), Dr. W. J. Scott, Messrs. F. B. Bucklaud, C. H. Burt, J.P., A. A. T. A`Vard (Post Master), Inspector Hudson (Royal House- hold Police), Chief Constable Carter (Windsor Borough Police), Superintendent Holding (forks Police, Clewer Division), Mr. .1. Canning, Mr. W. E. Herbert, Mr. J. P. Hollis Mr. W. J. Miley, Mr. C. Sainty (Waterworks Manager), Mr. S. W. Mellows (Sanitary Inspector), Mr. A. Sainty (Old Windsor), T,ispector Taylor (N.S.P.C.C.), Inspector Verrall (R.S.P.C.A.), Messrs. R. %V. Houfe), S. Osmond, J. Moir, A. H. Atkins, P. Robinson, A. Jacobs, Gilbert, F. C. Chipchase, J. Fleet. C. Kelsey, G. Charlish, Sec.—The presentation consisted of a handsomely illuminated address and a cheque for trs.

The Mayor made the presentation and the Town dlerk read the address as follows:

"To Martin NiehoNs, late Chief Constable of _ _ _ Windsor.

"During the six years that you occupied the position of Chief Cronstable of this Borough, the residents of the town and neighbourhood have been deeplyimpressed by the keen sense of justice and impartiality displayed by you whilst carrying out the duties of your office. The kindness shown by you on all occasions to the deserving poor, and the assistance which you so readily rendered to various charitable organisations have been sincerely appreciated by those with whom you have come in contact. We heartily congratulate you upon having been elected to till the office of Chief Constable of Warrington, although -we regret your resignation which such appointment has necessitated. We ask you to accept the accompanying cheque as a token of the esteem in which you are held, and we hone that you and yours family may have health and happiness in the future, and that you may long be spared to remember the time that you have spent in the Royal Borough of Windsot.

. JAMES T3RowN, Mayor." Chief Constable Nicholls, in returning thanks. said it was customary on occasiont of that kind to apologise for one's inability to put into words what one would like to say, and he for himself felt quite incapable of adequately returning thanks for these handsome gifts. The Mayor had mentioned the fact that he (the speaker) had helped his Worship and other Mayors during his six years in Windsor in connection with the distribution of alms to the poor, but he could assure them that it was quite a labour of love. (Apnlanse.) Whilst he was in,Windsor he endeavoured as far as be could—and none of them were infallible—to give satisfaction to 1 everybody. (Hear, hear.) Some people said that was a wrong principle, but he had always felt that in going through life it was more desirable to try and give satisfaction than to do otherwise. (Hear, hear.) During the time he was in Windsor, he received the greatest, kindness fram everybody—from the Corpora- tion, Magistrates. Officials, and the general public, He made every effort to return that kindness in Avery way he could, and to judge from those kind gifts be had been successful. He could assure them that he had very pleasant memories of Windsor. It was a bright spot in his heart that never would he wiped out. 4onlange 1 He could never forget the pleasant time 1. spent there. No Chief Constable could have been bettor treated by a Watch Committee than he was by the Watch Committee or the Windsor Town Council. They alwtys helped and supported him in every way; and he mi;ht say that without the support of a Watch , Committee it was qu:te, impossible for a Chief Con- stable to carry out his duties adequately. (Applause.) He received every courtesy possible from Windsor people,and while at Windsor he always tried to do what was right and just, it was never his desire to persecute anyone; he would rather help them; and prevention of crime was better than the detection of it. (Applause.) The position of Chief Constable of a police force was a delicate one, and it was difficult to give satisfaction to everyone, but he had tried. He was quite unable to express his feelings to everyhody for these handsome gifts, and he hoped he should always be held in their esteem in Windsor. His wife would be pleased to hear of what had taken place that day. He again thanked them from the bottom of his heart for their kineness to him, and the kind treatment he received during the time he was in 'Windsor. (Applause.) The addrss, which was much admired was, the work of Mr. Stephen M. Wyborn, Windsor. It is richly illuminated in gold and-colour, designed in the style of thirteenth Gothic. A conventional floral design in colour upon a gold ground forms the border, with the Borough coat of arms worked in the centre, the top corner °nom-wing a view of the Castle and other views of the Town Hall and Police Station. The name "Martin Nicholls "is prominently displayed in richly- illnmiaated colours and forming the head-line of the address written in German text, to which is attached the Mayor's signature. Following the address are the names of the subscribing members of the Corner ition printed in vermillion and the names of other subscribers in blue. The writing and printing were lined in gold, and the capital letter: illuminated in various colours and huraished in gold. The adlress is enclosed in a handsome oak frame.

Fire In Turner-street.

At two o'clock on Sunday afternoon a fire broke out in a shed belonging to ilamblett Bros., Turner-street, in which a large quantity ~f -miscellaneous goods had been stored for sale. The fire brigade, under the direction of Superintendent Turner and a full cotnple- ment of men, with the steam fire engine " Captain,". were soon on the spot and succeed.d in extinguishing the flames in about an hour. Fortunately the roof was of corrugated iron or the loss would have been very considerable. The damage is not covered by insurance. The origin of the outbreak is not known. Several horses were rescued frail an adjoining stable.

••••••+em•so " LAW " AND "GOSPEL"

Tt is related that a Scotch minister chanced to meet two of his parishioners in the office of a lawyer, whom he regarded as being too sharp. The lawyer jocularly and not very graciously put the question: "Doctor, these are members of your flock; may I ask, do you look upon them as black or white sheep?" "I don't know," answered the divine, drily, "whether they are black or white sheep; but I know if they are here long they are pretty sure tip be well fleeced." HARRY COPP.

ENGLISH

A twelve-year-old school girl recently pre- sented the following composition to her teacher. It would be a good exercise for our young readers to turn into correct English. C. "A right spite little buoy, the son of a kernel, with a rough round his neck, flue up the road as quick as a dear. After a thyme he stopped at the house and. wrung the belle. His tow hurt hymn, and he kneaded wrest. He was two tired to raze his fare, pail face, and a feint mown of pane rose from his lips. The made who herd the belle was about to pair a pare, but she through it down and ran with all her mite, for fear her guessed would not weight; but when she saw the little won, tiers stood in her eyes at the site. 'Ewe, poor dear. Why do you I.ye hear? Are yew dyeing ? ".Know,' he said, I am feint.' .She boar him inn her arms, as she aught, to a room where he mite be quiet, gave him bred and meet, held a cent bottle under his knows, untide his choler, rapped him- up warmly, gave him a suite drachm from a you, till at last Jae went fourth as hail as a young hoarse."

I MEANT TO. I did not rise at the breakfast-bell, But was so sleepy—l can't tell— • I meant to. The wood's not carried in. I know; • But there's the school-bell, I must go. I meant to. My lesson I forgot to write, But nuts and apples were so nice. I meant to. I forgot to walk on tiptoe; Oh, how the baby cries! 0! 0! • I meant to. There, I forgot to shut the gate, And put away my book and slate. I meant to. The cattle trampled down the corn. My slate is broken, my book is torn, I meant to. Thus drawls poor idle Jimmy Hite, From morn till noon, from noon till night:. I meant to. And when he grows to be a man, He heedlessly mars every plan With that poor plea, "I meant to." EMMA C. HOW EDDIE PREACHED.

"When I get big enough I'm going to be a preacher," said Eddie, one day. "What is a preacher ? " asked grandma. Eddie looked surprised. "Don't you know what a preacher is? A preacher is a man who tells people what the Bible means. And he says, Thirdly, my brethren,' and everybody listens to him. It's nice to have people listen to you." Grandma smiled. "I- think you are big enough to preach now," she 'said. "Really and truly, grandma?" asked the little boy, eagerly. "Yes, really and truly." "I'm 'fraid not," said Eddie, after a few minutes of thought, "or I'd know how, and I don't."

"What does the preacher do first?" asked grandma. "He takes a text and then 'splains it. I can't do that."

"Oh, yes, you can," said grandma. "Here is a good text for you to explain: Be ye kind one to another.'"

"There's nothing to 'eplain 'bout that," said Eddie. "You just be kind to everybody, and that's all there is of it."

"A good text, though, for my little preacher's first sermon. I should like to have him preach from it for a week." _

"Preach a week? Why, grandma, I can't." "Can't you be kind to everybody you meet for one week?"

Eddie looked thoughtful. "Would that be preaching?" he asked. "It would, and the very best kind. A good preacher has to preach in that way, or people will not listen to what he says in the pulpit." "Well," saki Eddie, with a sigh, I suppose I can try; but I wasn't thinking 'bout that kind of preaching." "You'll be showing everybody what that verse in the Bible means, you know," said grandma.

"It's not kind to the teacher to whisper in school," said Eddie, the next day; and he did not whisper once. "It's not kind to Bridget to play along the road and keep my dinner waiting, either"; and he hurried home from school. - -

"It's not being kind to mamma when I don't do errands promptly," he said; and he did quickly and well whatever he was bid. Every day and all day he thought about what was kind, and tried to do it. The end of the week; came. "How do you like preaching?" asked grandma. "Why, I like it; but, grandma, I guess every- body has been preaching 'bout that text, for everybody has been so kind to me,"

THOUGHT HE MIGHT DO,

Mulligan. a strong, powerful labourer, was seeking a job. He did his best to persuade the clerk of the works, but the latter pointed him to the board bearing the words, "No hands wanted." Mulligan looked at the board, and said, "That may be so. You may not need any more hands; but can you use me feet, now that your steam roller's broken down?" HARRY Corr.

"A little incident in my garden, I think, m 33 be interesting to your readers," writes a cor- respondent. "A starling had been caught by the leg in the eaves under the roof of my cot- tage; and, judging by its pitiful cries, was in great pain: Its struggles attracted five or six other starlings, Who at once started to search for worms and other food, with which they fed the little prisoner, evidently thinking that thi, would in some way alleviate its pain. HONESTY.

A nobleman was once asked for a trifle in.the• High-street of Edinburgh by a little ragged boy. He said he he no change, upon which the boy offered to procure it. His lordship, in order to get rid of his importunity, gave him a piece of silver, which the boy conceived was to be. changed, and ran off for the purpose. On his. :eturn. not finding his benefactor, whom he ex- rected to wait, he watched for several days in :he place where he had received the meney. At length the nobleman happening again to pass that way, the boy accosted him, and put the change he had procured into his hand, count- Ing it with great exactness. His lordship was so nleased with the boy's honesty. that he placed him at school, with the assurance of providing for him.

Professor De Candolle, of Geneva, has re- cently brought together many interesting facts tbout the latent life of seeds, and their ability to withstand very low temperatures. After re- calling instances in which coeds have sprouted after lying apparently dead for hundreds of years—in one case 1,500 years—he gives the re- sults of experiments on subjecting seeds to a freezing cold. Corn, oats, fennel, and some flower seeds were exposed during 118 dayti to a, emperature of 40 deg. Fahrenheit below zero. kfterward, when placed in suitable surround- ings, nearly all of the fennel, oat, and corn iceds, and many of the others, germinated. He oncludea that the protoplasm, or the principle .)f life, in a resting seed, is in a state of inac- tion not comparable to that of a smouldering Lire. but rather like that of a chemical mixture. which is capable of forming a combination, whenever the required conditions of tempera- ture and illumination are prteent.

LARGEST CRATER ON THE EARTH. The volcano Aso-san, in Southern Japan, on the Island of Siushu, possesses the largest crater known on the earth. It is about four- teen miles across in one direction, by ten or eleven in the other, and is surrounded by walls of an average height of 2001 t. Al- though the volcano is still active, its erup- tions consist only of ashes and dust. In- deed, a range of volcanic mountains, evi- dently of subsequent formation, extends directly across the old crater. In these par- ticulars Aso-san resembles some of the craters of the moon, where a long history of successive and gradually enfeebled outbreaks of volcanic force is graphically represented.

FACTS ABOUT STEEL.

Microscopic study is adding much to our knowledge of the properties of steel. It has been shewn, for example, that there is an im- portant difference between steels rolled, or annealed, below a temperature of about 750 deg. Centigrade and those annealed at higher temperatures, which are thought to have been overheated. They do not endure " fatigue " so well as those annealed at the lower temperature. The permanent and in- jurious microscopic strains are more minutely subdivided and more uniformly distributed in the less heated steels, and this fact is re- gairded as explaining their superior ability to endure "fatigue." ,

===== 0002601_19071019_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
BOILING-POINTS OF METALS.

One of the last contributions to science made by Henri Moissan, the French chemist, was a classification of the boiling-points of metals. His experiments with the electric furnace demonstrated that there exists no known solid which cannot be volatilised in the laboratory. In the meantime other ex- perimenters have shown that every gas, ex- cept perhaps helium, can be liquefied, and even solidified. Moissan found that certain families of metals are peculiar with regard to volatilisation. The platinum family, for instance, has a very high boiling-point, much higher than that of gold. Manganese is the most volatile; nickel comes next; iron is re- fractory to a high degree; and molybdenum and tungsten have the highest boiling-points in the entire list.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
SCIENCE AND CHILD LIFE

During the last quarter of a century sta- tistics have shewn a notable increase of the average expectation of the length of human life from birth. It appears that within 100 years this average for most civilised coun- tries has grown from twenty-eight to fifty- three years. The statistics vary for different countries, but all shaw an advance. How- ever, as the French scientific journal, La Nature, points out, this does not mean that the chances have been evenly increased throughout the duration of life, but rather that they have been enormously increased during gne period of life, namely, that of in- fancy, and to a smaller degree in after years. Owing to the progress of hygiene and medi- cal science, the causes of infant mortality have been largely eliminated, the reefs at the commencement of the voyage of life are in the main avoided, and the average length of life, counting from the start, appears increased for all.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
WATER SCORPIONS,

In the Biological Bulletin Mr. S. J. Holmes has some interesting notes on the habits of young "water scorpions." These creatures, it may be noted, are not really scorpions, but belong to the class Insecta. The species studied is Ranatra quadriden- tata, a native of America. One of the peculi- arities of the water-scorpion is the death. feint. When in this state it may be cut in two without giving any response. Shaniming death, as this phenomenon is often called, is usually thought to be an instinct

EVOLVED 'BY NATURAL SELECTION for the protection of the species.- In the case of the American species, however, which rarely leaves the water of its own accord, while the death-feint is only manifested when the insect is in the air, Mr. Holmes con- cludes that it is "rather an incidental result of certain physiological peculiarities of the organism than an instinct which has been built up by natural selection for the benefit of the species." The author finds a similar difficulty in accounting for the strong attrac- tion which light has for this insect. In the air near a bright light it becomes wrought up b the highest pitch of excitement in its efforts to reach it. No useful purpose can be ascribed to this curious behaviour.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RADIUM ATOM.

One of the chief difficulties in; the study of radium is the minute quantities of this ele- ment with which chemists have to work. And even these minute quantities are ex- tremely difficult to obtain, and the neces- sary purifications are long and complicated. Another difficulty in the way 'of obtaining exact data is that the element itself haa never been obtained, but only certain of ite compounds, such as the chloride and bro- mide. In these circumstances it ,is a triumph of manipulative skill to have determined with any degree of accuracy the atomic - weight of radium. When some few years agc Madame Curie first determined it, she had at her disposal for this purpose only nine-hun- dredths of a gramme of highly-purified radium chloride. With • , THIS MINUTE QUANTITY she made the ne,cessary determinations, and found the atomic weight of radium to be 225, Comparing this figure with those asSociated with the other elements, we find, says the Globe, that the atomic weight .of radium ex- ceeds all the others except those of thorium and uranium. Of these the former is 233, and the latter 240. Now according to the modern view of the atom as made up of electrons, elements of high atomic weights, by dis- charging certain of their electrons , give rise to elements of smaller atomic *eights. Hence thorium and uranium are regarded as the parent elements of radium.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
COLOURING GEMS.

The entanglements of sunlight, which pro- duce the rich colourings in • certain precious stones, demonstrating that , even such hard substances as '.(crystals blossom forth at cer- tain epochs as radiantly as flowers in the most beautiful garden, are, we know, due to the agency of vibration. The spectrum, 14 dividing what is called white light into its constituent parts, produces all the prismatic harmonies of the rainbow. • The ruby makes permanent, in the most vivid manner, one of the upper partials of the fundamental Jane- tint. Sapphire retains for ages the azure of the heavens. The emerald fixes a permanent portrayal of the purest verdure which grati- fies the eye at the height of summer. But now the German iiavant, Mr. Miethes, in his " Annalen der Physik," demonstrates that, by means of

EXPOSURE TO RADIUM, the hues of pricions stones, excepting the ruby, can be altered. The influence of radium-liromide will turn a white diamond lemon-colour in eight days. In another eight days the light lemon be,comes a bright chrome. This applies to the Kimberley but not to the Brazilian stone. In two. days a light-blue Ceylon sapphire becomes a light yellow, and afterwards reddish yellow. Then it approaches the chestnut brown. Subse- quent heating causes these various shades to transpose themselves back again, but a yellow tint is permanent.

MR. CR9SFIELD, M.P. AND THE RAILWAY CRISIS.

The Directors Behind the Times.

'**Vk

Sir,—Referring to a letter published in last Sat ur day's issue of your contemporary, the " Guardian," I will gladly answer here the question put in that letter by Nall Secundus." lam the more glad to do so because I should be very sorry if he, or any other friends of mine among the Railway men at Warrington were to misunderstand my attitude in regard to the present Railway crisis. -.A.b )ut the middle of September, during the early stages of the controversy, a representative of the London" Daily Chronicle " wrote and asked me for an interview on the matter. At that interview I expressed an earnest hope that the Railway Directors would at any rate agree to a preliminary conference, because that would obviously give both sides the best chance of arriving at a settlement. I also emphatically expressed my adherence to the general principle of collective bargaining, and my belief that it was applicable to the Railway. These opinions were published in the London" Daily Chronicle," and I rather think they were quoted in the "Examiner." Again in Manchester on September 21st, when speaking at Belle Vue, I, in effect, urged the advantage of collective bargaining, and put the question, which has so often been put recently—awl ncrer cto,accrerl why, if this system can be adopted with safety and advantage in a great National service like the Pot Office, can it not be adopted with equal safety and advantage on the T?ai/leay4 ? So I had no notion there could be any doubt as to one's position. flowerer, while the speeches at this large gathering of Liberals at Belle Vue were well reported in the " Examiner,' they were I think almost, if not entirely, ignored by the "Guardian." I do not mention this at all by way of complaint of any policy ad )pted by yout contemporary, but perhaps "Nulli Secuadus" will let ma o:fer him a friendly word of caution. Because I am afraid that if this little incident —the suppression in the " Guardian " of the speeches at Manchester on the Railway question and other questions of. the day in rather startling contrast by the way to the prominence recently given in that journal to Protectionist speeches by Tariff Reformers—if this is a criterion of what we are to expect in the future " Nulli Secuadus " will scarcely be able to keep himself acquainted with Liberal opinion in the town if he trusts solely to the information he gets :as a reader of the "Guardian.' But in this case I am very glad to be able to post up " Nulli Secundus," and to clear his mind of any doubt by giving him (and all who are interested in this great and grave issue) an emphatic assurance as to my very strong opinion about the dispute. The whole nation is of course concerned in it, and consider- ing how (in a very literal sense) vitally concerned, I think one must be struck with the moderation of the language used in the press and on the platform. People have felt, no doubt, that they could not reader a worse disservice than by helping to inflame passion in any quarter 30 long as there still rernanel hope and belief that a inotitts rirendi should, could and must in some way or other be arrived at. And everyone hopes that still. Suggestions have been 'nide, for instance by the " Manchester Guardian," which I will not prolong this letter by discussing, and in the last resort it remains to be .seen what Mr. Lloyd George may be able to do. But meantime the sands are running in the hour glass, and after interminable, and to my mind, all things considered, unpardonable delay the Directors maintain just the same stiff, unbending, non possumuB atti6ide they have adopted from the outset. The most charitable thing that can be said of them is that they seem to be about a couple of centuries behind the times, but if they have really made up their minds not to yield an inch to anything but force, that, I respect- fully submit is very dangerous, and, all things con- sidered, an unpatriotic position to take up. One still hopes for the best, and that saner counsels will pre- vail ; but if Mr. Lloyd George has to intervene he can only do this to good purpose with a stiong backing of public opinion, and it is to be hoped, and I think very confidantly anticipated that this will not be wanting—at all events in our part of the country. Our main position is, I think pretty clear. We hold that:—

(1) All experience goes to show that in the larger organised industries collective bargaining tends, not to encourage strikes, but on the contrary, to get rid of them and to promote industrial peace. (2/ That while in the complicated conditions of modern industry, and not least in regard to the complicated terms of payment on the Railways, when an alteraticn of wages is discussed, Directors have the benefit of the information and assistance given them by officials, managers and sub-managers of the company—that is to say, of the best experts they can get —it is absolutely fair and only reasonable that the employ 63 of the Company should also have the assistance of experts competent to argue their case fairly, and be in a position of sufficient independence to enable them to do so. (3) That broadly speaking, Capital is fortun- ately quite well able to defend its legitimate rights, and that this is frequently done by combination, and that, on the other hand, combina- tion is just as necessary, and just as legitimat.i in the interests of labour.

(4) That the Directors' contention that this Railway Union—including 100,000 employees— ,is superfluous, and that the men can get all they are reasonably entitled to by direct application to their chiefs, is contrary to the fact that at the present time in many cases the hours worked and the wages paid are not fair.

Without prolonging this letter by discussing other points raised in this controversy, I venture to enter-
