A CHILD'S DEATH. VERDICT OF "MANSLAUGHTER" AGAINST THE MOTHER. Coroner and the Doctors.

The inquest which had a somewhat sensational open- ing last Vriday was resumed at the Warrington Police Station yesterday morning, when Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, and a jury inquired into the circum- stances attending the death of John Kelly. aged 13 months, son of John and Catherine Kelly, 21, lorris Court, The parents of the child who are at present serving terms of imprisonment for neglect of their children, were brought from prison to attend the pro- ceedings. 'Alt% Browne represented the N.S.P.C.C. and the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) was also present. There was a large attendance of the public. It will be remembered that last Fridly the Coroner commented severely on the delay that had arisen in notifying him of the death.

Inspector Cox, of the N.S.P.C.C., said that in con- sequence of the inquiries he made the father and mother were brought before the magistrates on Thursday, the 3rd inst., charged with neglecting deceased and another child. The man was sentenced to a month's imprisonment and the woman to three months'. The husband was addicted to drink when he could get it. The woman was of filthy habits and addicted to drink. The Inspector detailed his visits to the house and pointed out that the condition of the children and the home became so bad that he warned the parents and for a time there was an improvement. In consequence of a complaint he again visited the house on October Ist and found the woman drunk in the kitchen. The baby John looked like a skeleton. Its body was very dirty, the head all matted with filth and the child was whining and appeared to be very ill. The family lived and slept in the kitchen, and the smell was foul and sickening. The woman used very bad and abusive language to him. There was some food in a dirty cupboard, but not a particle of baby's food to be seen. Dr. Peacock° was called in by witness and found that ths baby weighed 111,1bs. in a shawl, whereas the standard weight of a child of 13 months was at the least 201bs. The child was removed to the Workhouse Hospital. He was of opinion that the children had been neglected in a gross and wilful manner, causing them unnecessary suffering and injury to health. When asked by Dr. Peacock° what the baby was fed on, prisoners said: " It has the same as we have." Asked what it had had that day, the mother said, "A tomato, some steak, and a banana." In answer to the Coroner, witness said: I think the mother is to blame. If she would be a better woman and look after the home and the father, things would be very different in the hon.se. On many occasions the man was sober when his wife was not ?—Yes.

Is she a millstone round his neck ?—Oh, yes. Tbere is no doubt about it.

Evidence was also given to P.C. Clough and Nurse Goodall as the state of the deceased child. Dr. Peszocke corroborated the evidence of Inspector Cox. When he heard that the child was dead, he told Mr. Awbery that the case ought to be reported to the police. He held a post-mortem examination in company with Dr. Smith, and found the body nothing but skin and bone. He thought that neglect had accelerated the death. _ _ _

The Coroner remarked that Dr. Peacocke had done his duty in the case, and the doctor's evidence was corroborated by Dr. Smith. Dr. Harold Joseph, who gave a certificate that the child's death was due to exhaustion, was questioned by the Coroner. _

When you gave the certificate were you aware that the father and mother had on the Thursday previous been, charged with neglecting the child and sent to prison ?—No I knew nothing about that. When you attended the child from Tuesday until its death on Saturday, was no history of the case given to you? Did nobody say the child had been sent there by Dr. Peacock ?—No. Dr. Peacocke's name was not mentioned at all.

Was not the name of the N.S.P.C.C. ?—No. It was presented to me as one case amongst a number of others. _ _

I think I may put it that if yon had had the informa- tion on Saturday, would you not have thought it your duty to have communicated with the police ? —Certainl v. The Coroner remarked that it was very curious that the child should be admitted to the Workhouse and nothing should be said to the doctor about the circum- stances of the case.

After a short adjournment, the jury unanimously returned a verdict of " Manslaughter" against the mother. They intimated that although they did not consider the father was free from blame, it was not sufficient to justify them sending him for trial. The Coroner said he thought the verdict was a most sensible one. The woman was committed for trial at the next Liver- pool Assizes on the Coroner's warrant.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. _ "The Prince of Pilsen." Lovers of musical comedy are admirably catered for this week at the Royal Court Theatre where the attraction is Baron H. Harriss's Company of nearly seventy artistes in the London success "The Prince of Pilsen." Large audiences have nightly: expressed their delight with the entertainment which is one of the smartest and brightest things of its kind on the stage. There ate delightful songs, pretty dances, and an inexhaustible fund of humour, and the piece is beautifully mounted. The music is of the most tuneful description and everything goes with a fine swing from the rise to the fall of the curtain. All the fun arises out of the adventures of Hans Wagner, a Cincinatti brewer on tour, who on arriving at Nice with his attractive daughter is mistaken for the Prince of Pilsen, who is also expected, and not at all unwillingly becomes the hero of the hour and the object of the most flattering attentions from the ladies. Presently the real Prince comes on the scene and, being reluctant to strike a discordant note, conceals his identity. He falls in love with Wagner's daughter at first sight and is in the act of stealing a kiss when her brother appears and challenges him to mortal com- bat. There are all sorts of amusing compli- cations, his Highness and the b )gus Prince in turn finding themselves in prison, but all ends in the most approved style. The comedy is presented by an excellent company, headed by Miss Sophie Harriss who as Mrs. Madison Croker, the fascinating widow from New York, could not be excelled. Her personal attractions are great, and she infuses a vivacity into her acting which at once commends itself to the audience. Mr. Parry Kilburn, who por- trays Hans Wagner, is responsible for most of the merriment, for he goes through some amazing escapades, but always comes out smiling. He is never at a loss for a joke, and some of his songs prove highly popular. As the Prince of Pilsen Mr. Frank Crimp achieves a notable success. He possesses a fine baritone voice and uses it to the best effect, a particularly pleasing song being "A passing cloud." Miss Connie Leon is the personifica- tion of gaiety and mischief as Sidonie, the French maid, and is repeatedly encored for her exceptionally clever dancing. Mr. Ernest Gifford gives a capital study of Francois and executes some wonderful manceuvres in complying with the many orders showered upon him. Miss Hazel Saqui does full justice to the part of Nellie Wagner, for she is a sweet singer and a graceful dancer. Mr. Bernard Russell, as Arthur de Willoughby Rosemoss, is a typical self-opinionated dandy, while Mr. S Lindon Lang as Lieutenant Tom Wagner, and Miss Ernestine Desborough, as Miss Edith Adams, are associated in some pretty love scenes. One of the most striking features of the comedy is introduced in the second act Iwhen various types of American girls —including the much-talked-of Gibson girl —are impersonated. There is a strong chorus and the orchestra is specially I augmented. NEXT WEEK'S P ROGR A NIME. "THE BREED OF THE TRESEIAMS." Our readers will be pleased to hear that next week at the Royal Court Theatre, we are to have the annual visit of Mr. Frank Bertram's Company in that excellent , play" The Breed of the Treshams." Those who have witnessed the play on previous occasions will remember the fine dramatic scenes and the splendid character of the Rat. it is Mr. Bertram's policy to engage a different actor each year for this most difficult part, and Mr. Sydney Bland who will play the stellar part at the Court Theatre,is by far the best Rat Mr. Bertram has been fortunate enough to engage. Schooled with the late Sir Henry Irving and Wilson Barrett, Mr. Bland has every qualification for the part. As before, Mr. Bertram brings a complete Company numbering 22 artistes and the entire production,scenery, costume and furniture. That was very sound and very comprehensive so far as it went, but we had travelled a good way past that in the constructive era in government, which after all was so so very much more difficult than the era of emancipation in which the older school of reformers, amongst whom he was brought up, had to play their part.He was afraid that looking into the future one could scarcely hope that in this complex era our city fathers would have less responsibility and less weight upon their shoulders, and therefore one was reminded of what was said by a nobleman of Berkeley-square,who was very brilliant but a little whimsical perhaps, who had done some good things and said some very wise thines, and this was one of the wise things—he had ' dwelt upon the satisfaction which people in localities might feel in seeing the fruits of their work reaped before their own eyes—(hear, hear)—and in seeing tangible results of their efforts which were so very much more palpable than in the case of the efforts made by people in Parliament. That fact must add to the dignity and importance of civic work and civic life. He was sure they all hoped that in the future everyone present would have good health and prosperity,and in proposing the toast which he had the great honour of submitting, that the Mayor during the continuance of his office would have health and strength and great success in the work that lay before him in presiding over that important Council. (Applause). The toast was honoured with great enthusiasm. THE MAYOR PRAISES THE COUNCIL. •

The NIA YOR. in responding said the office of Mayor was by no means an easy one to fill in this growing and important borough. When he took up the reins of office nearly twelve months ago he only did so on receiving the assurance of the members of the Council that they would assist him in maintaining order in their deliberations and in upholding the dignity of the Council. That promise, he was happy to say, the mem- bers had fulfilled, and he bad had a very pleasant and comfortable year. In view of the fact that the Council had done him the honour of of asking him to take office for a further year, he thought he might, without attempting in the slightest degree to flatter himself, feel that he had carried out his duties to the satisfaction of the members. (Applause.) He looked forward to the coming year with no misgivings, believing as he did that with a united Council, working wholly for the good of the inhabitants, improvements would continue to be made in the good old town of which they were so proud. The Mayor concluded with a hearty acknowledgment of the invaluable help extended to him by the Mayoress. "TOWN AND TRADE OF WARRINGTON."

The MAYOR OF BOLTON, who was introduced by the Mayor as an old friend of his from his native town, proposed the toast of" The town and trade of War- rington." One of the first essentials of municipal govern- ment, hesaid,was that of sanitation. Some years ago he was struck with the number of dreadfully narrow streets in Warrington, and he was glad to see that the Town Council had risen to the occasion and proceedEd with schemes for widening the thoroughfares. He would only give them this advice : "In widening your streets make them, wide enough." (Hear, hear.) Do not let them half do it. (Hear, hear.) Warrington was well situated for trade and commerce, and they must keep the rates of the town down so as to encourage manu- facturers to invest their capital and find employment for the workers. (Hear, hear). Alderman BENNZTT in responding alluded to the recent historic pageant at Liverpool. Warrington, he said, had a history more illustrious than Liverpool and he hoped some day we would hold a pageant more glorious and magnificent than the one which took place at the great port. He refused to set any bounds to the growth of Warrington. Its future, he thought, might excel that of almost any town in this part of the country and he did not see why in a hundred years Warrington should not be as big as Liverpool. He proceeded to strongly advocate an increased use by Warrington traders of the Ship Canal and said he would not be satisfied until WA had a dock and until Warrington was a port of call. It was also very desirable that the industrial advantages of Warrington should be impressed on manufacturersand with that object in view the Chamber of Commerce had in hand the publication , of a commercial guide to the town. If the municipality was to be increasingly extending its functions, one thing was vital and essential, and that was that the work should be conducted in a thoroughly businesslike way. If the extension of enterprise meant the endless repetition of the slums which were a disgrace to War- rington and to every civilised community, he did not desire extension. He wanted progress to be coupled with greater prosperity and happiness for all classes of the people. Warrington had been passing through a long, dark, dismal era of agitation, bitterness and unpleasantness. He was glad to think that was passing away. This week he had heard ominous rumours of mysterious compacts and dark plots which seemed to threaten the peace of the Council, but he refused to believe in them. They had had quite enough of recrimination, bitterness, party spirit and politics in the Council and he believed all the memners were now animated by one unanimous desire to sink all those minor differences and go forward with the one idea of the progress and improvement of the good old town. (Applause). Councillor 134r,r, also reponded. They could, he said, derive inspiration and encouragement from Warrington's past history and at the same time rejoice that that there were arising in the town to-day men who would carry forward worthily the banner of reform, progress and achievement. All present were united in their regard and respect for the town in which they lived. In speaking of its trade, they had need to be thankfnl that our manufacturers and businessmen had taken advantage of all the researches and discoveries of the last century with the result that the products of Warrington were known the wide world over. As one who bad been born and bred in Warrington, he took an honest pride in its past and an honest interest in its future and when our traders and manufacturers were beginning to wonder how further they might develop their enterprise he hoped they would not always look at the rates and wages bill, but that they would sometimes look at the terrible burdens they paid away yearly for no reward and no services rendered. He wished all manufacturers, all those who by the work of their own hands and brains sent produc- tive wealth abroad, would see tha t they were not burdened in future by toll and incomes that were never earned. He wanted the Council to realise that the truest wealth any nation could possess was in the healthy, happy-hearted•men and women that it produced. (Applause.)

"THE CORPORATION." MR. H. ROBERTS AND THE CORONER.

Mr. Henry ROEIF.RTS in proposing the toast of "The Corporation" said that was an occasion on which all differences were sunk and men joined together in hoping that in the days of the future one effort and one effort only should be made for the uplifting of the populace with whom they were associated and for the careful and honest administration of the laws which were entrusted to them and for that progress which after all was said and done, however they might differ in their views, they all desired to accomplish. Old politician and party man as he was, he did not altogether agree with the remarks which had been made that night in regard to party politics and municipal government. If municipalities had to frame their policies by any shaping of legislation, there might be something to be said in favour of maintaining a certain amount of party polities in that association, but when he reminded them that their laws were made for them and that all the Council had to do was to administer them in a spirit of broad catholicity, remembering all the interests at stake, he was bound to say that he failed utterly to see in what sense or degree party politics had anything whatever to do with municipal administration. The responsi- bilities which were put on municipalities were greater to-day than they had ever been and the future promised that the burden should be greater still. It therefore made it incumbent on all men who brought to the public service whatever duty they felt imposed upon them, that they should come to that service determined not to consider any interest of a personal or party character but to bring to the administration of the laws entrusted to them a feeling of fair play and justice and a determination not to move to the right hand or to the left but to do that which their knowledge and intelligence taught them to be right on behalf of those whose interests were so much in their hands. He believed in spite of differences which had existed in Warrington and which would exist for all time more or less, there was to-day quite as strong and keen a desire on the part of those who had the administration of the town's affairs in their hands to do justice to those affairs and to behave honestly in regard in regard to them, as ever there had been in the history of the town. Mr. Roberts paid .11. high tribute to the Corporation officials and offered his congratulations to the two latest officials—the medical officer of health and the chief constable— (applause)—both of whom had come to War- rington with the highest credentials and large experi- ence and, he believed, with an honest determina- tion to do their duty. He was glad to see the Chief Constable looking so well as he was. (Laughter.) He was somewhat afraid that possibly the remarks made in what should have been considered a judicial Chamber a few days ago might have hurt him. However, he seemed pretty well, and he (Mr. Roberts) had not any doubt that Mr. Nicholls would be able to give a com- plete answer of a perfectly satisfactory nature in regard to an attack made upon him in his absence in a very upindielal manner by a man who ought to have remem- bered that great as his powers were—and they were almost unlimited—therefore the greater should be the discretinn with which be used them. Public men, con- cluded Mr. Roberts,might get the,plandits of the public. but nothing would give them such satisfaction as would came to them after years of earnest work, if they felt they could look back upon the work they bad done in connection with the Corporation and say to themselves "This work I did honestly, uprightly, according to the best of my knowledge and ability, and I leave it with a conscience void of offence." (Applause). Alderman TINNION responded and said they had a Council to-day which seriously and honestly tackled its work and felt its full responsibilities. He was glad that the spirit of unkindness and want of coortesy and consideration that some members of the Council

It was a very rash promise seeing that although he had attained the mature age of 59 years, he had never proposed a toast before. (Laughter) After consideration he had come to the conclusion that his task might be a very easy one as he had known Mr. Crosfield all his life, although perhaps Mr. Crosfteld did not know it. (Laughter.) He had always taken a kindly interest in Mr. Crosfleld's welfare, and after all that was not a political meeting. It was rather a scene of peace, and he might say plenty. (Hear, hear.) They were far removed from the scene of political strife, and they saw only a genial, high-minded English gentleman —a member of a worthy family, long and honourably associated with the town and its social life—and one who at length had succeeded to the proud position of Member of Parliament for his native town. MR. CROSFIELD'S RESPONSE.

POLITICS IN MUNICIPAL LIFE.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, M.P., who was received with cheers and the singing of "For he's a jolly good fellow," said he was afraid he could not adequately thank them for their kindness and cordiality without seeming affectation, but he could assure them he thanked them extremely for such a kind reception. He supposed politicians as a rule when they heard anybody talking about them expected to he slated a little. (Laughter). They certainly got accustomed to it and perhaps the contrast that night was rather pleasing—at any rate it was particularly gratifying when such courteous, genial and kindly observations were made about one by a political opponent. (Hear, hear). He was sure, as Conncillor Dr. Joseph said, that if on other occasions and on other fields little political rivalries would occur, under the Nlayor's genial presidency and at that festive board all political differences were, fortunately, entirely dissipated. (Hear, hear.) He thanked them very sincerely and cordially for their kindness, and while it was very pleasant to reply for oneself he was very lucky that night in having the doubly pleasant duty of proposing the health of his Worship the Mayor. (Applause.) That certainly was both an honour and a pleasure, because everybody in that room knew that while they need not make invidious comparisons and talk about breaking records—(laughter)— the Mayor had been absolutely indefatigable in his attention to all duties in the town, large and small. lie (Mr. Croslield) had not the privilege of knowing personally, but he knew from what many friends had told him, of the admirable way' in which his Worship had presided over the the work of the Council. (Applause.) He had never spared himself in furthering every good cause in the town and in guiding the Council's deliberations he had shown not only dignity but a most admirable impartial- ity. (Hear, hear). That impartiality was a great thing where, as in so many other Councils in England, they could not keep politics entirely out of the Chamber. (A voice: They ought to be). In his younger days he could remember hearing many a discussion on that point, because Mr. Sylvanns Reynolds and his (Mr. Crostield's) father were continually debating that very question as to how far politics might or might not he kept out of the Council. He suppesed everybody would agree that in municipal government it would be a great evil if party politics were carried too far. (Hear, hear.) In one sense and in a larger sense, although he could not pretend to any great experience of municipal matters, it appeared to him that one could not keep politics altogether out of municipal work, and for this very simple reason—that he must say it struck him that they could hardly think of any measure introduced into the imperial Parliament which in one way or other in some &arm did not depend for erficient and satisfactory working on the wisdom shown by the municipal government. There was hardly a law, he thought, to which that did not apply to some extent. Take the much debated question of education to which Mr.Bracecarno had alluded. That was under the control of municipal bodies and they saw municipalities in so many different ways nowadays grappling with the problems that concerned child life. Then on the question of public health. municipalities were engaged in seeing that food was inspected and drainage properly attended to, that they had a good water supply, and that infectious cases were isolated, and in a score of different ways they had the great!question of public health dealt with by municipal authorities. Parliament could not do it without them and that was where it seemed to him that in sone aspect. of the matter politics must to some extent enter into municipal government, but only in one salute. They might say with regard to a matter that Alderman Bennett and others were interested in, that in so far as it affected the body politic the unexampled nuisance, became it was nothing else, of ! black smoke was a political matter. That certainly was one of the most far-reaching questions that affected the democracy of our country living in the towns ; but he ' only mentioned these things to show that even on the question of smok'e Parliament could not do anything unless mnnioipalities chose to exercise the powers that were given them. Therefore the thesis seemed to hold good that imperial politics must depend on local effort for satisfactory working. After all the whole thing seemed to illustrate one very big fact and that was that alike in Parliamentary work• and in local government, there was a vast distinction to be drawn between the work of fifty years ago and the work of to-day. He had quoted the discussions between his father and Mr. Sylyanus Reynolds on one topic and he would allude to another in which he remembered Mr. George Crosfield took a very great interest, because he was one of the older school of Liberals and was very fond of quoting a book written by Buckle called "The history of civilization "—(laughter.) There was one passage in that hook whieh he (Mr. Crosfield) turned up the other day and found it heavily scored by his his uncle. It described the duties of Government in a

"THE YEOMEN OF THE GUARD."

PERFORMANCES IN THE PARR HALL.

There has been a renewing of happy associations at the Parr Hall this week. When amateur opera first cast its spell over Warrington audiences four years ago, it was a delightful production of "The Yeomen of the Guard " which stamped the Musical Society's experiment a complete success. Since then we have been treated to some of the lightest of Gilbert and Sullivan's collaborations, and public appreciation has been unmistakable. " The Yeomen of the Guard," however, stands on a plane of its own, the serious dramatic element alternating most effectively with humour and gaiety, and it therefore appeals to one with peculiar power. There was a natural desire on the part of many patrons of the opera to see this masterpiece again produced by our local amateurs, and the committee thought that this year presented a favourable oppor- tunity. It is no mere platitude to say that this week's performances mark an advance on the original effort. The improvement is striking and cemplete, and everything goes with that smoothness which comes of ripened experience. With only two exceptions, the principal parts are in the hands of those who sus- tained them on the first occasion, and there is not a weak link in the chain. The high reputation of the Warrington amateur operas is enhanced and the Society has every reason to be proud of a presentation of " The Yeomen of the Guard " which we believe would compare very favourably with any similar undertaking of the kind in the country. In none of the wonderful series of works does the genius of Gilbert and Sullivan find higher expression than in " The Yeomen of the Guard." There is sparkling wit, bright, ear-haunting music, yet under- lying it all is a deep note of pathos. We are in turn amused by the irresistible cornicalities of Jack Point, the merriest of jesters, and touched by the cruel change of fortune which makes him one of the most miserable of men. Throughout there is the strange blending of the joyous and the saddening, and the succession of events enacted within the shadow of the grim old Tower of London hold the audience enthralled. The opera is realised in the most attrac- tive of settings. We are carried in imagina- tion to a bye,gone age when young gallants were kept in durance vile on trumpery charges of scorcery, and those picturesquely attired individuals known as Yeomen of the Guard had exciting times. But the good old days were not with- out their rollicking pleasures, and when the merry- man and his maid appear and dance and sing for the entertainment of a group of happy citizens, the scene is full of animation. There are moments, too, when the intensely dramatic dominates all else, notably when, at the climax of the first act, the headsman and his assistants make ready for the execution, the wierd tolling of the funeral bell is heard, and instead of the prisoner appearing, yeomen dash in with the astonish- ing news that he has escaped. The finale of the opera is remarkable for the play of conflicting emotions, and one's feelings are divided between sympathy with the inconsolable Jack Point and rejoicing with Elsie Nfaynard in the consummation of her heart's desire. It is due to the performers to say that they make the most of these powerful incidents, the effect being so realistic as to create a profound impression. Jack Point is of course the centre around which everything turns—indeed it is generally admitted that he is one of Gilbert's finest creations. Mr. C. P. Gough again achieves a triumph in the part, which he portrays as to the manner born. He has all the gaiety and freedom that should characterise a strolling jester and his dancing is extremely clever, The Gilbertian humour is safe in his hands, and the only fault we can urge againtst him is an occasional indistinctness of utterances and a tendency to drop his voice at the end of a sentence. Mr. Gough shines equally in the more doleful passages—he is the personification of despair when occasion demands— while in the song "Oh ! a private buffoon is a light- hearted loan" he gives us so piquant a recital of the trials of the jester's calling as to be cordially encored nightly. On Wednesday evening owing to the somewhat sudden indisposition of Mr. Gough it was necessary to requisitionsthe services of his understudy in the part of " Jack Point." That Mr. J. Forrester was able to respond to this call at such short notice was a matter for both surprise and admiration. Not only did Mr. Forrester fill the breach, but he played and sang the onerous part of the Jester with conspicuous ability and success, demonstrating thereby that the Opera Committee has ample reserves of histrionic and vocal talent. Mr. Forrester's clear ennunciation of the spoken words, his acknowledged abilities as a vocalist, and his obvious determination to do his level best under the circumstances, carried him through to a most successful finish, and earned for him the audience's warm plaudits and several encores. Mrs. Arthur Booth, as Elsie Maynard, has a finely dramatic role, and her interpretation is a great advance upon her past achievements. She acts with befitting grace and charm, while her singing is in the best of taste. Her powerful and flexible voice finds full scope in the stirring ballad " 0 weary wives," which she gives with both feeling and dramatic power, a repetition having to be con- ceded at each performance. The singing farce " The Merryman and his maid," in which Mrs. Booth and Mr. Gough are associated, is a deservedly popular feature of the opera, the quaint and wailing refrain captivating the audience, with the inevitable result— an encore. Mr. J. Rabbitt is another favourite with Warrington audiences and he excels himself as the dashing Colonel Fairfax. He fully realises the possibilities of an attractive part for which he is in every way fitted, and his vocalisation is at once soul- ful and artistic. It is a treat to bear him in the songs " Is life a boon ?"and "Free from his fetters grim" and the delight of the audience has been manifested nightly in enthusiastic encores. Miss Mabel Henshrill is an ideal Phcebe Meryll—vivacious and coquettish—and it is no exaggeration to ascribe much of the success of the opera to her charming and natural portrayal of this character. She is thoroughly at home on the stage and her sweetly expressive rendering of the ditty " When maiden loves" sung at the spinning wheel immedi- ately on the rising of the curtain, at once ingratiates her with the audience. Her performance throughout is highly meritorious and has met with war nly expressed approbation: One of the most ludicrous of Gilbertian characters is Wilfred Shadbolt, head jailor and assistant tormentor, and Mr. W. H. Wallington's conception of the role could hardly be improved upon. The studied melancholy of his demeanour contrasts so sharply with the fascinating gaiety of Phoebe, whom he ardently woes in a blundering sort of way, that one cannot but he amused, while his utter help- lessness in face of the artful persuasiveness of this fair charmer, gives rise to situations which have an important bearing on the opera story. Phcebe's song " Were I thy bride," given with such piquancy while dexterously relieving the admiring jailor of his keys and restoring them again without arousing his suspicions, is one of the most delicious pieces of cajolery imagin- able, and each evening this inimitable piece of singing and acting has been rapturously encored. Another richly humorous item is the very original and tuneful "Cock and hull" duet in which Jack Point and Shadbolt share the honours, the jailor being suddenly galvanised into unwonted activity and making grotesque efforts to imitate the nimbleness of Jack Point. Mr. J. W. Bibby comports himself with befitting dignity and austerity as the Lieutenant of the Tower, his perfect composure, fine presence, and clear enunciation combining to make his impersonation of the character all that conld be desired. Mr. F. Fieldhouse scores a distinct success by his able portrayal of the kind-hearted Sergeant Meryll, taking the part with a calm confidence which is as essential as it is appropriate. Miss Garnett Ball figures for the first time as Dame Carruthers, and despite a little nervousness and stiffness, is largely successful in the part, her rich contralto voice being used with effect in the stirring song " When our gallant Norman foes "which with its blood-curdling chorus is listened to with deep interest. The Pretty duet " Rapture, rapture," in which the love-sick 'Dame rejoices at having secured the hand of Sergeant Meryll,—much to the chagrin of

the latter—is one of the brightest things in the opera. Mr. J. E. Hannan, who is also included in the cast of " The Yeomen of the Guard " for the first time, has only a small part as Leonard Meryll, but he makes the most of it, the refreshing vigour and dash which he infuses into his rendering being deserving of all praise. Minor parts are capably sustained by the following: —Kate, Miss Alice Daunt; headsman and assistants, Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, R. 11. Silcock, T. Baxter and W. Turner; first yeoman, Mr. J. Parkinson ; second yeoman, Mr. W. Clay; first citizen, Mr. G. E. Guest ; second citizen, Mr. W. Moores. Of the many attractive concerted items, one of the choicest is the trio and chorus " To thy fraternal care "—in which Miss Henshall, Mr. Fieldhouse and Mr. Wallington are associated—for in addition to the sparkling music there is some delightfully humorous by-play. The beauties of Vie well-known madrigal "Strange adven- ture " are well realised by Miss Ball, Miss Daunt, Mr. Rabbitt and Mr. Fieldhouse. The number is to a great extent unaccompanied and perhaps the excite- ments of a " first night" were respinsible for a very perceptible decline of the pitch in Monday's inter preta- tion—a defect which has been remedied during the rest of the week with the result of a nightly encore. Another highly acceptable trio is that commencing "A man who would woo a fair maid," by Miss Henshall, Mrs. Booth and Mr. Rabbitt and the succeeding quartet " When a wooer goes a wooing," as sung by the above-mentioned vocalists with the addition of Mr. Gough, is a gem of harmony, the mixture of gaiety and pathos producing a telling effect. There is a strong, judiciously selected chorus, the voices being well balanced, and the impressive choral numbers which are so prominent and pleasing a feature of " The Yeomen of the Guard " receive the most adequate treatment. The Society is fortunate in having a very complete amateur orchestra, which, led by Mr. R. H. Pearson, does yeomen service and, under Mr. Crossley's conductorship, shows a thorough graso of the score. It would, however, be an advantage if the accompaniments to the songs were a little more subdued. At times the audience found it almost impossible to hear the words of the soloists, owing to the assertiveness of some sections of the instru- mentalists.

Unstinted praise must be accorded to Mr. F. H Crossley, the Society's able conductor, for his indefatigable and tactful labours on behalf of the opera project. His enthusiasm in the work of the Musical Society is unbounded, and that the production of the opera has reached such a stage of efficiency and completeness is due in no small measure to his untiring efforts. Nor must we omit paying a tribute to the services of Mr. Foden Williams, who makes his first appearance amongst us as dramatic " coach " and stage manager. His wide experience in amateur operatic work has been carefully and conscientiously utilized for the benefit of the Warrington enterprise and he has every reason to be proud of the success which has crowned his works. _ _ _

- The whole of the arrangements for the opera have been carried out by a Comn:ttee c-msisting of fessrs. H. Bond, J. W. Bibny, F. H. Crossley, W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., and J. Newcombe, the last named gentle- man being the hard working and genial hon. secretary and treasurer.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Services for Sunday.

Bold-street Wesleyan Church : Niorning 10.30, Rev. Sidney Pitt; evening 6.30, Rev. Thomas \filler. Golborne-street Baptist Church : Nforning 10.45, evening 6.30, Nfr. James Hodgson (student Baptist College, Manchester). Baptist Church, Latchford : Morning 10.30 and evening 6, Rev. J. F. Nfatthews. Wycliffe Church, Bewsey-street : The harvest thanks- giving services will be held on Sunday and the preacher both morning and evening will be the Rev. John Yonge. Special hymns and anthems will be rendered by the choir.

Bewsey-road We3leynn Church: Morning 10.30, Rev. Thomas Miller; evening 6.30, Rev. Sidney Pitt, when the subject will be" The launch of a Liner: A lesson for the young."

Latchford Weslayan Church.

Nt the harvest thanksgiving services held on Sunday the preachers were the Rev. E. J. Gawne and Ur. Hick- man. Good congregations assembled and special anthems were sung by the choir. In the afternoon a cantata entitled r• Two Seasons" was rendered by the choir. The solo parts were taken by Miss Mabel Henshall (contralto), Miss Violet Brown (soprano), Mr. Clayton (tenor), and Mr. Warburton (bass). Mr. Enoch G. Oldbury was the conductor, and Mr. W. H. Ford officiated at the organ. Mr. Atkin was in the chair.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay.

The harvest thanksgiving services in connection with the above Chapel were held on Sunday. Mr. George Hunter, Mayor-elect of Leigh, occupied the pulpit morning and evening, and in the afternoon n service of song entitled " Farmer Brown's Testing Time " was rendered by the choir. The connective readings were effectively given by Miss V. M. Taylor. The chapel had been tastefully decorated for the occasion, and good congregations assembled at each service. The collections amounted to £7 7s. 7d. Mr. F. Atkinson presided at the organ, and Mr. S. Millington conducted the singing.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Latchford Baptist Church.

The Rev. John Francis Matthews preached the anniversary sermons on Sunday at the Latchford Baptist Church. At the afternoon and evening services the church was crowded. The Apollo Male Voice Prize Choir, under its able conductor, Mr. Harry Berrey, pro- vided a musical procramme for the afternoon which was forcefully rendered, and gave great delight. " Where is He ?" and "The Destruction of Gaza '— pieces rich in dramatic power and expression—showed the capacities of the choir. Mr. J. R. Conway took the solo parts. Miss Woodland and Miss J. Armstrong also contributed solos with pleasing effect. The organist was Mr. Albert Trow. The offerings for the day were 211.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission.

Harvest fo-stival services were held in this Mission on Sunday. The morning service, which was for children, was conducted by Mr. Christopher Eccles, who gave a helpful address. In the afternoon the ser- vice was conducted by the pastor, Rev. H. Wall, while the Rev. J. Yonge spoke on "The cultivation of the thankful spirit." The speaker placed " thankfulaess " in the forefront of the Christian graces and urged all his hearers, both at harvest time and on all other occa- sions to endeavour to manifest the spirit of a cheerful and willing gratitude for all God's mercies. The speaker at the evening service was Mr. E. Wynne Jones, and the building was filled to its utmost capacity. A fruit banquet was held on Monday evening, at which a short entertainment was given,concluding with a sketch entitled " Mrs. William's Will."

===== 0002601_19071019_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
Stockton Heath Wesleyan Church.

The Rev. R. Wilkins Rees, of Fleetwood, and formerly of Warrington, preached the anniversary sermons to large congregations at this Church on Sunday. in the afternoon a service of song entitled "A Rough Diamond: Peter Mackenzie," was given by the choir in the presence of a large congregation. Mr. F. E. Penketh conducted, and Miss Nl. Miller presided at the organ. Ar. W. Hickman occupied the chair. Special anthems were sung at each service by the choir.--On Monday evening a fairly large congregation assembled to hear Mr. Rees' popular lecture. "The Famous One-eyed Preacher of Wales." Mr. R. H. Meldrum presided. Mr. Rees gave some highly interesting reminiscences of Christmas Evans, the famous preacher. When the preacher began to speak, he always produced a small phial from his pocket, wet the tip of his fingers, and passed them over his blind eye. It was laudanum, to ease tbe excruciating pain he often suffered. He undoubtedly possessed what Lord Beaconsfield said Dean Stanley possessed, "a picturesque sensibility." He was a great preacher and people walked miles to listen to his sermons, and they were always amply repaid for their trouble.—At the close Mr. Brereton proposed a comprehensive vote of thanks to the lecturer, organist, choirmaster, choir and everybody who had assisted to make the anniversary services such a success. Mr. Whitfield seconded, and the motion was carried. The collections at all the services were in aid of trust funds.

===== 0002601_19071019_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
Meetings for Men and Women. " The Bible Still Supreme."

In spite of the heavy rain a good audience was present at the meeting for men and women in the Golborne Street Baptist Church on Wednesday evening. The Rev. J. F. Matthews lectured on "The Bible still Supreme." He pointed out the wide range in time covered by the various books together called the Bible. It had helped to mould and fix the form of English written and spoken speech, as Luther's translation of the Bible had done for German. The place the Bible took in litera- ture was not second even to the position assigned to it in human affection. It was read and studied and used by all sorts and conditions of men. It was unexcelled by any other similar collection of religious books for its variety of form and substance. Writers and lecturers who found in it an armoury for assailing churches, and Christians, were never more widely known than when engaged in their piercing criticisms of its alleged contradic- tions, whether it was Tom Paine. Robert Ingersoll, Charles Bradlaugh or Robert Blatchford. Where then, asked the lecturer, lay the Bible's supremacy? They would be able to give different answers ; but he submitted four reasons for the view he took about which he thought little obinction could be taken. First : The Bible was scrupulously faithful

FOr Chronic Chest Complaint, Woods' Great Peppermint Cure. 1/1b 2/9.—Advt.

-4111NA4P'.

to human nature. Its portraits always and everywhere were faithfully drawn. It covered nothing up, offered no extenuating circumstances in its references to, and ' judgment upon,conduct.lt went even further,for secondly, it traced the defections of man to a moral gangrene,and fixed the measure of his personal responsibility. The heart was deceitful above all things and desperately wicked. The novelist, the philosopher, and the dramatist stopped short at the exhibition of the villainy of the villain; but the Bible looked on him as a possible aubject for, and capable of, redemption. There lay the difference. Thirdly, amidst all the melees, the chaos, and confusion, and the disorders of the human soul the Bible definitely taught and preached that through all, God had been pleased to make himself known and I seen and felt. God had nowhere left Himself without a witness, "but in every nation 'he that feared Him, was accepted of Him?' Fourthly, Mr. Matthews said no other book so faith- ! fuly recorded the evolution of the idea of God until it I reached its climax—(l) in Christ's declaration of the I Universal Fatherhood of God, Who loved the whole world : (2) in the objectivizing and personalizing of I that love in Jesus ; and (3) in the testimony with which expen ience bore out the fact that God was in Christ, reconciling the world unto Himself. So the ! Bible became a lamp unto their feet and a light unto their path. It was the inspiration of their finest youth, the strength of their strongest manhood, and the com- fort and delight of their old age. Let them read it regularly, studs, it carefully and critically. They would ' find it pointed to the true light which ligheth every man coming into the world.

Here Butterworth got hold and cleared to centre with a fine kick. The visitors would not be denied, however, and ie the course of another attack Tilley marked in the Runcorn half, and Thomas nearly placed a goal. Then Runcorn had a turn, and Deakin dashed for the line at the corner, but Tilley brought him down and cleared in brilliant style. Short Ipassing by Warrington took the game to the Runcorn I quarter, and a loose rush ended in Kenyon getting over, but be was whistled back by the referee. A mark by Watton temporarily relieved the homesters, but they could not hold Warrington forwards and were soon depending again. Jenkins got away on the right and I kicked high towards the goal. Jolley misjudged the I bounce of the ball which struck the cross bar, and iDickenson rushing up at top-speed took it right under the full back's nose and dashed over the line. note was a great cheer from the Warrington supporters, which was renewed when Thomas placed a goal. Runcorn made a desperate effort after this and G. Williams burst through at centre but when faced by Tilley, he kicked and the full hack charged the ball. Half-time, Warrington 5 points, Runcorn nii. On resuming the game was very vigorous. Runcorn did some good punting and tested Tilley severely, but the Warrington back was very safe. Padbury nearly got in at the corner, and was damaged in the effort. Watton, Butterworth and Williams passed smartly but'were pulled up in the Warrington half. Scrummaging followed near the line, the Warrington men rushing through with Thomas to the fore and clearing to centre. Here Deakin made a bad catch and Kenyon snapped the ball up in a twinkling. The Warrington wing made great run and beating Jolley for speed raced round the posts with a flue try. Thomas again wetted, and Warrington were assured of victory. Runcorn tried bard and Jolley kicked a penalty goal from short range. Warrington kept up a strong attack, Jenkins running to the home quarter. Here the ball was heeled smartly and quick handling by Lees, Isherwood and Kenyon resulted in another smart try to the latter. New Zealand *Warrington A. ... 2 7 25 Salford St.Barts 0 0 0 New Zealanders at Widnes. Tile All Blacks made their initial appearance in Lancashire on Wednesday, when they played Widnes. The home team were without their strongest forward, George Aspey, and his place was taken by Argent. There were several changes in the All Blacks' team, Messenger, Smith, and Todd being notable absentees. There would be about 7,000 spectators present. Teams : Widnes—Jones, back ; Barber, Lambert, Taylor, and Davies, three-quarter backs ; Lally and Noon, half backs; Argent, Aspey, Carr, Evans, Rigby and Turner, forwards. New Zealand—Turtill, hawk ; Rowe. Wrigley, and Wynyard, three-quarter backs ; McGregor, five-eighths; Kelly, full hack ; Trevarthen, Tyler, Tyne, bile, Cross, Wright, and Pearce, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Renton, Hunslet. Touch-judges, Messrs. F. Turner, Warrington, and J. IL Houghton, St Helens. Playing with the advantage, of a strong wind•\Widnes at once attacked, and the ball went dead. Smart work by tbe visiting backs carried 'play into the home territory, and Wynynrd was accounted for twice when near the line. The first score came from a scrummage near the home line. Kelly passed the ball out to Wrigley, who beat several of his opponents, and scored wider the posts. The same player failed at goal from an easy position. The All Blacks returned to the attack, and a mull by Davies let in Wynyard, with a clear field, rind be was never overtaken. Wrigley again failed at goal. For a time the game was pretty evenly contested, and then Barber beating Roe e, scored at the corner. Davies failed at goal,but a few minutes later made amends by placing a goal from a mark. Just before the interval %,'l•igley placed a penalty goal. Half-time : New Zealand, 1 goal and 2 tries, to Widnes 1 goal and 1 try. The first-hell had been very evenly contested. The All Blacks got posstssion more often than their opponents, owing mainly to the fact that they played seven forwards. Early in the second bait the New Zealanders attacked, and a pretty bit of play by Rowe ended in McGregor crossing the line. NVrigley placed a goal from a somewhat easy position. Widnes, livened up somewhat after this, and a beautiful bit of passing and repassing by Alf Taylor and Lambert ended in the latter crossing the line just at the corner flag. Davies failed at goal. The Widnes three-quarters exhibited some nice passing, but it was altogether too slow. On the other hand, the New Zealanders' hacks vier° very fast. Wright dashed away by himself, and literally carved his way through his opponents, and scored near the posts. Wrigley again failed at goal. The New Zealanders were now having by far the best of the game, and their backs were always dangerous.. Rowe picked up from loose play, an just before he got to the line he gave the ball to ,Wynyard, who had an easy run in. Wrigley again failed at goal. Barber was collared, but lost the ball, and Tyler picking up ran in a sixth try. This time Tort ill.took the place kick, and landed a goal. A few minutes later NVidnes were penalised, and Trirtill kicked another goal. Just before the finish Widnes pressed, and Davies scored an unimproved try at the corner. Results :—All placka 4 goals 6 tries (26 points), Widnes 1 goal 3 tries (11 points).
